downward spiral that began several years before the
war with the United States and Great Britain. Living
conditions are becoming inoreasingly drab, and economio
shortages are becoming more acute, while the publio
vacuum regarding knowledge of external conditions and
complete popular support for the war continues the same
as ever.
As time goes by foreigners are more than ever
isolated from the Japanese, and Mr. Ciuntu apologized
for not having any striking information to give since,
not possessing any official status and having refused
to accept the post of Rumanian Minister offered by the
Antonescu regime, he was certainly not in a position
to attempt to learn anything unusual.
The War and Propaganda
Today in Japan there is no such thing as politios
in the Western sense of the term for the entire nation
is devoting itself to the supply of the war machine.
The people maintain the same steadfast attitude with a
belief in final victory, and there is no open grumbling.
The Emperor continues to make his usual number of
appearances at military celebrations and all the great
national holidays.
Since the fall of the Tojo cabinet, however,
Japanese public speakers and the press have constantly
emphasized the growing difficulties of the war and the
probable length of the struggle. The speeches of the
former American Ambassador to Japan, Mr. Grew, are quoted
by the Japanese press to show that the United States also
realizes that the war will be long and hard. However,
this office's source met no single well placed Japanese,
outside of the military with which he did not have con-
tact, who really believed in a clear cut victory for
Japan. All of these people, although not certain how
it would be brought about, expected some sort of a
compromise peace of the type they felt followed the
Russo-Japanese war.
It is interesting to note the observer's view that
the Japanese public, at least until the American
air raids began in the last few months, somehow considered
the war a bit remote. The only outward signs of the
war were declining living standards and the increased
drabness of life. However, the first shock the
Japanese public received was the loss of Saipan. Two
Japanese police officials were sent to question the
source, who was asked whether in his view this loss
constituted a menace to Japan. In the press and in
statements the civilian suicides upon Saipan were given
great

=== Page 3 === (confidence 1.00)
-3-
great prominence, but the Japanese expressed the view
that these civilians had been foroed to commit suioide
by the military.
Propaganda against the Americans takes the form
of always using the adjective "beasts" in referring to
them. The press continues to state that Americans
fight only for adventure and money, and, to prove this,
various salaries and allowances of American airmen are
published in the papers.
Air Raids and Treatment of Americans
The Japanese have been making preparations for
American air raids for some time, and, when the super
fortress raids began, the people were conditioned to
the possibility of these raids, and their personal
behavior was competent.
One of the means that the Japanese adopted to pre-
pare for bombings and to avoid large scale fires was
to tear down many of the flimsy residences around key
industrial areas. The source thought this had been
done in some of the important districts and had visual
evidence of this being done in June 1944, when houses
were torn down for a distance of from fifty to sixty
yards on each side of the Tokyo-Yokohama Railway tracks.
Mr. Ciuntu had some talks with the Swiss in Tokyo
and met a Swiss Attache in Korea while he was returning
to Rumania via Russia, who gave him additional details.
This latter person, Mr. Angst, is well and favorably
known to Americans formerly in Japan, and he stated to
Mr. Ciuntu that he considers the Japanese are treating
interned Americans better than they first did and that
their living conditions are improving. He proposed
the question as one he was unable to decide: whether
the Japanese attitude was changing toward the American
prisoners because they were beginning to understand them
better or because they considered that it was to become
a long and serious war for Japan.
Industrial Mobilization and Economic Shortages
Women are being used more intensively in Japanese
industry than ever before; so much so that conservative
Japanese are afraid that the traditional family system
and its concomitant, subjugation of women, will be
disrupted by the women gaining their economic inde-
pendence. This office's source admitted, however, that
he had seen no outward signs himself of any such
changes.
The mobilization of people for industry has pro-
ceeded to such an extent that in Tokyo during the
day

=== Page 4 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
day the streets are virtually empty because everyone is
busy in some form of war activity. Japanese families
are not permitted to have more than one servant sinoe
the others must go into war industry.
Economic shortages are most apparent. On the Ginza,
for example, an estimate of 60 percent of the shops
are closed and what stores are open sell such indigenous
products as silk and porcelain. The quality of "sufu"
fabrics is worse than ever. There is no soap, no
sugar, and fish is but rarely available since fishing
boats do not have fuel. Paper is extremely scarce, even
writing paper, and the newspapers continue to reduce
their pages. The NIPPON TIIES has four pages, and the
English edition of the OSAKA MAINACHI has but one sheet.
Tobacco is extremely difficult to find. There are but
a few taxis available, and these are for foreigners at
the Imperial Hotel, to transport luggage to and from
the station, the foreigners themselves either walking
or taking rickshas. No shoes are sold, and soles are
of pressed paper. Japanese must bring their rice
ration tickets to restaurants when they eat, and the
quality of restaurant food has greatly deteriorated.
Foreigners living at the Imperial Hotel are virtually
obliged to eat there because the quality of the fixed-
price meals in public restaurants is so bad. The food
in the Imperial Hotel also is quite poor. As an extra
treat a bottle of beer is made available once weekly
on the hotel menu. A very small black market for
conmodities is reported which deals exclusively with
foreigners since the Japanese penalties are so striot.
On this market sugar could be secured at loo Yen a kilo.
Prices are continuing to be strictly controlled.
Inflation is successfully being avoided, and all
inducements to excess spending are being rigidly
supervised. There have been no movies for a year.
The Takarazuka Theater has been closed, as has the Kabuki
Theater. There are a few Japanese traveling theaters
on tour throughout the Empire.
Fuel is a great problem, and at the beginning
of this winter a small bag of charcoal was the ration
for each family, with the Government announcing that
in mid-winter another small ration would be issued.
Last winter there was no heat in the Imperial Hotel
and this office's informant was forced to wear his
gloves and overcoat constantly.
Shipping
The informant is convinced that Japanese shipping
losses are the direct cause of the growing economic
shortages in the country. He considers that even the
Japanese themselves realize that these losses have
been

=== Page 5 === (confidence 0.98)
-5-
been heavy. In the big ports virtually no ships are
to be seen, but he considers that the principal traffie
is concentrated at the Naval stations and the smaller
Inland Sea ports. It is virtually impossible to secure
passage on a ship going south, so consequently travel
in that direction is done by plane. When this office'
source left Japan for Fusan in Korea, the ships orossing
the strait traveled only by day and took a zig-zag
course. At the time he made the trip there were two
ships crossing together accompanied by two small
escort vessels and patrolling planes overhead.
As an example of Japanese shipping losses the
informant cited the case of the requisitioned French
vessels, which were leased to the Japanese with the
agreement that in the event of loss the Frenoh would
be repaid the value of the ships. At first, said
French friends, the Japanese did pay for ships lost,
but now they continue to pay rent for the remainder,
even when the French know some of these have been
lost, since it is a military secret to admit these
losses and since it is cheaper to do so.

1. The following information was received from a Rumanian civilian who
spent 16 months in Japan up to 28 November 1944. Source appears to
be well informed and a keenly intelligent reporter of fact.
2. Civilian morale of the majority of the Japanese is very high. The
people are uninformed about the truth of the war and Japanese naval
defeats, but appear to believe their leaders implicitly. Respect for
authority and veneration of the Emperor and military dommanders is
as great as ever. The morale of the soldier is very high. The pop-
wlation was very quiet during the Tokyo raids of 24 and 27 November with
no hysteria.
3.
Industrialists, professionals and others of the upper classes are not
confident in the ultimate Japanese victory. They have given up hope
of a separate peace between Germany and Russia, but believe that after
Germany is defeated Russia may interwene for peace between the United
States and Japan.
There is an absolute blackout in the cities. Theators are closed
and streets deserted after 9 o'clock in the evening. A few large
air raid shelters exist in large Tokyo office buildings. The
typical Japanese shelter is a small narrow trench, roofed over with
planks and dirt.
5. During the raid of 24 November which lasted two and a half hours
the population was completely calm. There was considerable Ack Ack
and Japanese fighters were seen. The Japanese claimed five bombers
destroyed. The raid of 27 November took place on a cluudy day and
the planes could not be seen. No Ack Ack was heard by source and
no claims were made by the press.
6. Source was informed of the following by an International Red Cross
representative: The treatment of American prisoners and the food is
much improved. Entertainment is now pormitted and no cases of
brutality are mentioned. The representative expressed the opinion
that the improved treatment resulted from a better Japanese understanding
of the American ways and habits. A large quantity of parcels from
the United States wms received in Japan and assurances were given that
they would be distributed soon. As a result the morale of american
prisoners has improved.
SECRET

7. Civilian transport is curtailed and first class accommodations have
been cancelled. A special authorisation is necessary for train
travel. Entry into and exit from Manchukuo is carefully controlled:
Foreigners and wivilians may enter on three days of each month only.
8. It is impossible for foreigners so ascertain who are the Japanese
leaders. The Prime Minister is subordinate to the Army and Mavy,
and all important transactions are determined by representatives of
the Army and Navy, together with a small group of business men and
technician experts.
9. Individual officials are either unwilling or unable to act on their own
authority, and all decisions are made by boards and committees.
10. All labor unions are suppressed, and only government-approved patriotic
associations are permitted. There is no talk, ever, of strikes and
the Japanese play ut American labor difficulities as evidence of
internal dissension in America.
1l. The Russians hamp.r Japanese diplomatic travel, particularly in
the case of couriers. Japanese diplomats are forced to wait for
months for visas to pass through Russia. ource commented that
the Japanese Diplomatic Staff in Rumania has not yet left for Japan
because Russian permits have not yet been issued. The Japanese
diplomats from Finland have reached Japan and were observed by source
passing the Russian-Manchukuo border on 7 December.

COMMUNISM AND THE FUTURE OF JAPAN
There can be little doubt that the Chinese Commmists
would like to see similar political processes ocour in
Japan and China. There is no concealment of the fact
that their ultimate goal for both countries is a Commumist
state. The power and prestige of Russia at the end of
the war make it even more likely that we may at some time
in the future face a Communist Asia.
Russian policy has shown itself to be eminently
realistic. Russia will probably not in the near future
openly interfere in the governments of either China or
Japan. If the Chinese Communists come into control of
a large part of China, as they are likely to do, and if
there is established in post-war Japan a form of
"bourgeois democracy", Russian policy will for the moment
have been satisfied.
Facing these probable developments in Asia, the
United States must likewise be eminently realistic. If
we adopt a negative, vacillating policy, we shall lose
the respect and friendship of all Asiatics and by default
shall lose our political influence among them.
In the case of Japan, we should not be afraid of
Communism. For purposes of political warfare, we should
use the Japanese Communists in and out of Japan. They
S
are a group which can and will help us. However,
politically they have been insignificant and it is
unlikely that they will rise overnight to dominate a
defeated Japan.
Americans cannot remake Japan. We shall inour only
MAR
resentment and failure if we attempt forcibly to impose
an American "way of life" on the Japanese. But we shall
also fail, and tragically, if we do as some publicists
have suggested, turn Japan over to the Chinese, or, to the
Russians.
Once
na
R
Cat.
Dist.

=== Page 12 === (confidence 0.99)
73
-2-
Once the military terms of surrender are satisfied,
we should be fair but firm in our dealings with Japan.
We should remember that, with a few changes, the Japanese
Constitution is as democratic as we could wish. Bewaring
of pseudo-moderates, we should encourage the widest
representation of Japan's social strata in her organs of
government. Beyond economic responsibilities, our most
significant task may well be to let the fresh breeze of
information into the stale, thought-controlled air of the
country. We should no longer shudder at the word "propa-
ganda" and we should not shirk our responsibility to
influence Japan culturally.
If this American influence is skillfully exerted
and, in the course of history, Communism still comes to
Japan, we shall at least understand its coming and be
prepared to meet it. The eventuality may bemither a
shock nor a menace. But if we have left the Japanese to
"stew in their own juice", Asia may again present
problems soluble only by force of arms.
John K. Emmerson

Letter from the Federal Capital
Japanese Parliamentarianism
BY DIPLOMATICUS
(The author of this article gives his own views, which are
not necessarily those of the publishers.)
It may take long, perhaps very long, until Japan will
be ready for "unconditional surrender." The capture of
Minila is certainly a long and extremely 1mportant step on
the road to Tokyo, and the suocessful bombing of Japanese
industrial plants and cities doubtless cannot be disposed
of with a shrug of their shoulders by the Japanese military
camarilla. But an infinite number of battles and heavy
sacrifices in blood will probably have to precedethe
occupation of the Japanese insular empire proper. In
addition, a large part of China has been conquered and
APR7 1945,
occupled by Japanese troops which will not give up the
country voluntarily.
The question whether there exists a Japanese "under
ground movement". and whether it will be possible to count
upon the establishment, or rather re-establishment of a
DOR
oonstitutional system in Japan, i.e. upon a parliamentary
government in Japanese style - upon "democracy" in a wera -
080
Soan be answered only on the basis of the historical develop
ment of parliamentarianism in Japan. I myself have had
opportunisi

=== Page 14 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
opportunities to make the acquaintanee of many osm
Japanese demoorats, Japanese parliamentarians, smer m
Jusuke Tsurmi, high officials such as the late Japanese
Ambassador Saito, 1ndustrialists and journalists. All of
them reverentially bowed before the picture of their "Rul
of Divine Origin", the "Tenno" (Mikado), but they were still
just as reverential supporters of the parliamentarian system
and have, in my opinion, remained it.
If one reads the book of our best offioial export em
Japan, the present Under Seoretary of State and former (frem
February 1932 to Pearl Harbor) Ambassador in Tokyo, Jeseph
Clark Grew, one cannot have any doubts about the faot that
the parliamentary system did funotion within the framework
of the Japanese constitution. It is worth while to delve
a little in the history of Japanese parliamentarian history.
From the vast literature, I select as a basis an excellent
2/
study which C. Burnell Olds published some time ago in Foreim
3
Affairs.
Japan received her first oonstitution in l889. It
could, of course, not be compared with "Western" constitu-
tions. It should not be forgotten that the Japanese nation
has an almost theocratio 1dea of its emperor, the Mikado, as
being a descendant of the Sun. With this idea is conneoted
the patriarchal order of state and society - an order
fostered by Chinese Confucianiem - according to whioh the
Son of Heaven, sent to rule over the earth, 1s not only a
descendant

descendant of the goddess of the sun, but also a dose
of a divine father of a family, the members of which
the ancestors of all those who up to this day have bel
to the Japanese nation. On this idea are based the 
sacrifices" as well - which are offered to the imperial
ancestors before all important ceremonial aotions of the
state, as the family, which has the obligation of offering
similar sacrifices; being in an intermediary position betwoem
the state and the individual, the family has in Japan the
legal character of an almost official institution.
According to the constitution of l889 government is
divided between the Emperor and the parliament. This
parliament consists of two chambers, the members of one
of whioh - distinguished by inherited nobility, professional
status, or taxable capacity - are determined partly by
birth, partly by special selection, and partly called by
the Emperor. All the members of the Second Chamber are
elected by the people through equal, secret and direot
voting procedures. The right to vote depends on the
liability to taxation, so that in fact only a fraotion of
the population can exercise its franchise.
Although the constitution of 1889 can, of course, not
be called an entirely democratic dooument, it was, however,
a step toward demooracy. The constitution explicitly
declared "the person of the Emperor sacred and inviolable"
and gave him all privileges of the empire, inoluding the
sanotioning of all laws, oalling and dismissing of the
Imperial Parliament, supreme command over the Army and Mavy,
the declaration of wars and the conclusion of treaties.
Th1o

=== Page 16 === (confidence 0.99)
not to be fashioned too different from the old militar
régime. The miracle was that at that time a comstitute
civio rights to the people - something entirely new - 
as freedom of residence, guaranties against arrest, detems
investigation or punishment without legal procedure, the
right to official court procedure by legally instituted
judges, freedom of religion, speeoh, and press, and the
right to public gatherings as well as that of petitioming.
As to the parliament, 1t had to be called every year, and
every proposed law had to have its approval; this inoludes
also approval of the annual budget of all inoome and expenses
of the state.
The first popular cabinet came to power in l898, and
the first popular party was formed in 190l. But this
Social Democratic Party attempted too muoh and was
suppressed almost immediately; the ideals for whioh it had
fought remained, however, and continued to be a thorn in
the flesh of those responsible to the government. At the
same time also the old military party was at work. The
Japanese embassies abroad reported the bellicose attitude
of the Western nations, their armies and navies. The
militarists shouted: "In this lies the secret of the power
of the Western nations. It does not lie in their peaceful
institutions and ideals, but in their superior military
organization. If we want to be able to resist their
encroachments, we must have an army and a navy which are
a matoh for any other nation whioh might oppese us." Due
to the influence of the military party Japan introdused
general compulsery service instead of the small number or
the previous "military specialists". New every man bees

Moreover, the belier in the celestial rigim or
of Japanese emperors, a belier which had recedel amte
background, but by no means been forgottem, was agm
the program with a kind of divine order, and even to make
it a religion.
In 1909 already, 1.e. only four years after the end
the Russo-Japanese war, the militarists suoceeded in ebtai
ing a law which made it possible for them to submit any
problem of military importanee direotly to the Mmperer, over
the heads of the parliament, the cabinet, or the Premier
Minister. But as soon as the Emperor had given his approval,
any kind of opposition was made impossible. Thus a double
government was superimposed on the state to the constermatien
of those who then already believed to recognize that such
a polioy would ruin the country.
The first World War brought the fulfilment of the
greatest wish of the war party, viz. a foothold in Ohina.
But although the militarists were now firmly in the saddle,
they had at that time seriously to take into account oppesi-
tion by the students and intellectuals. Although this
political trend was radically fought against by the
militarists and punished by the police, it spread more and
more. A new wave of democratio spirit developed in l918
with the establishment of the first concentration cabinet
under the leadership of Premier Minister Hara. Another
1mportant new factor was the 1noreasing influence of the
capitalistio businessmen, which greatly disturbed the
militarists, because the military budget had now te be

Foreign Minister Baron Shidehara succeeded in contributimg
much to the softening of the stirred-up passions of the
people. In 1927 he was forced to resign, and Baron Tamaka
became Foreign Minister. During his régime the well-known
"Tanaka Memorandum" was promulgated which set forth Japan's
program of world conquest in full detail and in expressions
which were by no means vague.
Hamaguchi came to power in 1930. But his agreement to
the 5:5:3 formula of the London Naval Treaty enraged the Army
and Navy. Never before had the civilian authority so
unmistakably ohallenged the army and navy with regard to an
important question of national defense. This action meant,
according to the militarists, that the cabinet had the right
to control Japanese foreign policy; it also enoroached,
according to them, upon the privilege of the Imperian Counoil
to influence the Emperor in all his 1mportant decisions.
Hamaguch1 was assassinated in 1931. On September l8th of
the same year the famous "Manchurian inoident" oceurred.
Since then the militarists have firmly held the helm.
Three abortive revolutions followed 1n l93l, a number of
prominent liberal men were murdered the following year.
In 1933 the Japanese Government left the League of Nations.
The influence of the people vanished gradually, but con-
tinuously from 1933 to 1936.
But even then demooratic idealism was by no means dead,
which was shown by various events during the last ten years.
One of them concerned a discussion whieh arose with regard

=== Page 19 === (confidence 0.98)
to Professor Minobe's theary on the emperor's relztith
the state. The books of the former were baamed, he
was foroed to resign from his position in the Upper
and to discontinue his lectures at three universities. In
spite of this new victory the "militant patriots" were far
from being contented, evidenee of which was the famous
"incident" of February 26, 1936. Assassinations and seeret
plots had become so numerous that the Emperor himself had
to 1ntervene by seriously reprimanding the army. This is
proof of the fact that the Emperor was by no means of one
mind with the military and would have kept them in cheek
if he had had freedom of action.
The parliament convened again on January 21, 1937 and
elected Hirota Premier Minister. The leaders of the Army
were accused in a stormy session of having violated the
constitution and the decrees promulgated by Emperor Meiji.
This led to the resignation of the Minister of War, General
Terauch1, and to the fall of the cabinet General Ugaki's
attempts to form a cabinet did not succeed, because no
militarist wanted to stay with him in the cabinet, although
the whole nation wanted him. Finally General Hayashi was
appointed, who was considered persona grata with his military
colleagues. But he had hardly entered upon his position,
when his government was most violently attacked in the
parliament by the veteran Ozaki, who very severely oriticized
the China polioy and the 1llegal seizure of politioal power
by the militarists. Ozaki's speech was received with very
great applause, but enraged the Premier Minister to such
an extent that he lost his temper and shouted: "In times
like these the slightest move sufrices to set ofr the
explosion." As a matter or fact, this explesiem happened
soon.
Maotako

=== Page 20 === (confidence 0.98)
Naotake Bato, who had just returned as an Abas
from France, entered the cabinet as a Foreign Mimibtes
and immediately took charge of a new conoiliatery peliey
toward China which, as he said, he wanted te pursme "with
the support of publie opinion." At the same time he
oriticized the attitude of his ohief, whose policy drifted
toward war, by saying that "ir the Japanese nation deeided
not to go to war, there would not be any war." A short
time afterwards the parliament was dissolved in a summary
way, which caused a storm in the whole country.
The replacement elections of April 30 were surprising
inasmuch as the number of votes was smaller than the year
before. The Proletarian Party, "Shaki Taishuto", achieved
surprising successes. Halr of the electorate in Kobe voted
for this party. It won 36 seats altogether. The elections
showed that the nation was obviously opposed to the cabinet
Hayashi, and that its resignation was therefore to be
expeoted.
Hayashi resigned one month after the elections and wás
replaced by Prince Konoye who had no diffioulties 1n form-
ing a cabinet entirely subservient to the wishes of the Army.
Events were now driving toward the olimax, although all
newspapers of the country strongly protested against the
governmental polioy in Manchuria whioh undoubtedly was bound
to lead to war.
On September 4, 1937 the Emperor appeared in the opening
session of the parliament. With regard to conduoting a war
against China he said in his speech from the throne; "The
war against China is being conduoted for the sole purpose
of making that country revise its attitude, so that peace
in Eastern Asia may be re-established as soon as possible.

=== Page 21 === (confidence 0.96)
a
He continued: "In view of this situation I expeet all
subjeots faithfully and in unity to serve the common
so that this goal can be attained." Nobody dared to make
any objection. The Emperor had spoken: God wanted it se.
In the same year a general suppression of all politieal
parties set in. The Proletarian and Farmers' Parties were
dissolved and their leaders imprisoned. A huge budget of
7500 mill1on yen was approved. There was no hope after all
these events that a general war could be avoided. The date
chosen was December 7, 1941.
In order to obtain a new system in Japan, the Allies
will have to make the following demands:
The present government, which is under complete control
of the militarists, must definitely disappear.
The continuous maintenance of the Emperor's sovereignty
is indispensable to the establishment of a real popular
government. Veneration of the Emperor and readiness
completely to submit to his will are rooted in the Japanese
character. There can hardly be any doubt - this is also
stressed by Ambassador Grew - that the interests of his
people lie at the Emperor's heart. Ir he abolishes the
military dictatorship by an imperial deoree and proolaims that
he re-assumes total sovereignty, the people will willingly
follow him.
In addition to the complete military defeat of Japan
and the re-investment of the Emperor with his absolute power,
the victorious Allies must insist on complete disarmament
of Japan and the return of all conquored territories.
Amendments to the constitution would have to make it impessible
that the military pursue their poliey without its being
known and approved by the public. Many of our Ameriean
experts on Japan believe that the attack on Pearl Warbor
0

=== Page 22 === (confidence 0.98)
teok plase without the government's appreval.
is shared by Ambassador Grew, who thinks that
Minister Togo did not know that war was imminent.
It will undoubtedly be objeoted - here I a fell
C. Burnell Olds - that the Japanese character, as shewn
the recent terrible events, is so bloodthirsty and ermel
that it will be impossible in the future to trust the
Japanese in any respeot. The only answer to this is that
one is not justified by cruelties to make indiscrimimate
accusations. One must lay the blame on those who are guilty
on the militarists, therefore, and not the entire nation.
The Japanese must be freed of their slavish subserviemee
to the military 1deology by military defeat, so that they
will always abhor conduoting war altogother. Then one will
be in a position to hope that the true Japan will rise
from the ashes.

FROM
Japan, Internal Politioal Situation, as
SUBJBCT :
analyzed in major artiole in February 4
issue of Red Star (Summarized in Mosoow's
843, Maroh 21.
1. Gist of the artiole: The fall of the Tojo
Cabinet in July, 1944 "showed that the 1dea of
military-Fascist dictatorship enjoyed no sup-
port even in highest Japanese oiroles". In-
clusion of prominent members of former political
parties in Koiso's Cabinet kindled hopes for
revival of the parties, disbanded in 1940 in
favor of the Imperial Rule Assistance Associa-
tion whioh failed of itself and through its
affiliates to reach the masses. Disappointment
over the course of the war has been reflected
in increased attention to the Diet as an organ
capable of refleoting publio opinion even in a
limited degree. Recent criticism of the Koiso
Government in the Diet and press is due not
only to military setbacks but also the Govern-
ment's laok of strength "to make a full break
with former reactionary political trends". The
war is ceasing to be popular with the Japanese
people who are no longer impressed by grandiose
war slogans; Japanese leaders are refleoting,
in utterances regarding Japan's being foroed
into war, a "natural desire to disolaim responsi-
bility for a war in which Japan will not be
viotorious".
2. Comment: There can be little doubt that
the article is correct in its thesis that,

=== Page 30 === (confidence 0.97)
Office MEmorandum - UNITED STATES GOVE
DATES
TO
-2-
FROM
SUBJECT :
since the fall of Tojo, there has been an
1norease in the influence of the elements who
favor less totalitarian procedures than he
advocated. However, this change was almost
certainly caused by the resistance of power-
ful industrial, business and financial ele-
ments who feared, as well as doubted the
effectiveness of, excessive government en-
croachment on the operation of the national
economy, rather than by pressure from the
general public, as the article seems to
imply. It may be added that, with the
present sharp deterioration of Japam's
military position, the pendulum seems to
be swinging baok toward greater regimenta-
tion by the Government of all aotivities
within Japan.

FROM: "NEAY INTELLIGENCE NO. 64,
Bupplement to GIEFANG RHBAO (The Emancipation Dally),
Publ1shed in YaNaN, 31 January 1945.
THe AIICAN LANDING UN LUZON AND THE KOISO CABINET. (By Yu LU)
On the 9th of this onth, American troops landed victor1-
ously on the Island of  UZON with a big and powerful forco.
Up to the l4th of the same month, they succeeded 1n taking
under control a seacoast of 45 iles in length and in thrusting
Into the inland 28 riles. They are now only several scores of
miles fi om MAnILA. In accordance with the news received, it
seems that the American troops have not yet recelved any kind
of large-scale and stubborn resistance from the Japanese.
This does not mean, however, that the Japanese are not will-
Ing, or are not capable of putting up a resistance; for they
understand quite clearly the importance of LUZON in the
defence of the Continent and JAPAn Proper. They will certainly
not ive up the 1sland without fighting hard. We can easlly
predict that the battle for LUZoN will be far more fierce than
that for LeYTe Island. The Japanese will resist the Amerioan
troops with all their forces and will make use of the weakness
of the long-distance lines of communication of the American

MEMORANDUM OF CONVERSATION
Moscow, Mareh S1, 1008
Present: Vioter Gutsuleseo
F. B. stevens
J. Davios, Jr.
Mr. Gutzulesco was formerly attache to the Rummim
where he stayed for one month. Frem Harbim he 
for Bucharest. Because of his difficult pesitien as 
former Rumanian diplomat, Mr. Gutsulesco requests that the
information which he provided be restricted to emly
American officials.
is already in American possession. It provides, hemever,
useful support of what has previously been reported.
American Air Raids. Mr. Gutsuleseo witnessed the
Doolittle raid on Tokyo. His comment was that this resd
had little effect on the Japanese beyond pessibly heishteming
their anti-American feeling. He was in Tokye during the
early B-29 raids which he believed to have had little mere
than nuisance value. Fires were .started but he was net
informed as to the full damage done. Foreigners were met
permitted to approach industrial areas so he was umable to
see the damage done in those sections of Tokye. He did
see one burned out residential section.
Tokyo air raid preeaution measures were imadequate,
Mr. Gutsulesco observed. Fire-fighting equipment was
insufficient with the result that the Jepanese were fomeed
for the most part to permit the fires to burn themselves
out. Air raid shelters were unsatisfactory. During the
early B-29 raids most of the population did not trouble to
seek shelter. Mr. Gutsulesco stated that the populaee was
given about forty-five minutes warning of approaching
American airorest.
While the initial B-29 attacks did not serve to shake
Japanese morale, Mr. Gutzulesco surmised that the subsequent
heavy attacks by fleet carrier aircraft and reinforeed B-29
formations must have seriously affected the Japanese.
Invasion Defense. The Japanese people now anticipate
a landing on Japan at any time, Mr. Gutzulesco stated.
School youths have therefore been conseripted. He said that
one sees quite a few young men in Tokyo who have been
inducted inte the Home Army. They were dressed in sumer

=== Page 102 === (confidence 0.98)
uniforms and he believed that they were inadequately
equipped with arms. Asked about coastal defenses,
Mr. Gutsulesco replied that sforeigners were prehibited
from visiting coastal areas so he had no infemmasiem m
that score. He had no doubt that the Japanese would
fight hard in the defense of their home islands but did
not feel that they were well equipped for sueh defemse.
American Prisoners. Mr. Gutsulesee stated thet
freight. He remarked that while they did mot appeer te
Japanese. For one thing, American prisoners received
supplementary food through the Red Cross and themefere were
probably fed better than the ordinary Japanese. The
Japanese press, Mr. Gutzulesco stated, reperted that a large
number of American prisoners were killed at Mukden during
an American air raid on that city.
Mr. Gutzulesco declared that eight American priseners
of war are working in the Tokyo radio broadeasting station.
He said that he did not know whether they were officers or
enlisted men, nor did he know their names.
War in General. While Japan was winning, Mr. Guteulesee
observed, Japanese prepaganda maintained that there wes me
connection between the Pacific war and the German war.
When the United Nations began to advanee in the Paeifie,
Japanese propaganda pointed out that the twe wars were
interrelated and implied that Japan might expeet te derive
help and benefit from a forthcoming German wietory. Mewever,
with German reverses becoming more apparent, the Japanese
propaganda line has again shifted back to the line that
there is no connection between the war in the Paeifie and
the war in Europe.
After the American landing on Saipan the Japamese
began openly to admit the danger of an invasion of Japem
Propor.
Asked about the possibility of Japan's suing for
peace,"Mr. Gutzulesco stated that the first rumors were
that after the fall of the Philippines Japanese peace
feelers might be put out. Later his Japanese contacts
suggested that peace proposals might be made after the
fall of Germany or after American landings on Japan.
The Japanese up to the time of his departure still
professed to be confident of their ability to defend their
home islands. They deelared that if the capture of se
small an island as Saipan presented the diffieulties which
it did to the Americans, successful invasion of Japan would
be an impossible task for the Americans.

When questioned regarding possible fristion metween
the Japanese army and mavy, Mr. Gutsulesce repkéed thes
that the army had up to the date of his departure sassess-
fully blocked the navy's wishes in this respest.
Morale and Propaganda. The enemy population,
Mr. Gutsulesco said, believed their govermment's propagande.
believed that Americans were savages and bent on he
destruction of the Japanese people. Whileithereswas no
evident breach in Japanese unity, Mr. Gutguleseo was of
the opinion that the people were undoubtedly war weary and
desired a termination of the hostilities. de heard no
comment to the effect that Japan's militarists were
responsible for the plight in which the Japanese people
found themselves.
While Mr. Gutzulesco was permitted by the onemy
authorities to keep his short-wave radio and so was emabked
to hear American broadoasts, the Japanese people were
prohibited from having any short-wave sets. They were
therefore unable to listen to American propaganda.
Food and Clothing. Food and clothing conditions in.
Japan, Mr. Gutzulesco declared, are "ten times werse them
in Mosoow". Acute food shortages, he said, have caused
obvious malnutrition among the Japanese peeple. The
Government attempted to explain the shoetage with stasements
that foodstuffs were being stored in Japan. This Oxplemasion
was not generally credited. In reply to a query as te what.
categories are most affected, Mr. Gutsulesco indieased that
the deficiencies appeared to be gemeral. He remarked that
there was a shortage of even tea, the Japanese peeple being
told that the Russlans had gone without tea and that there-
fore they. should be able to drink hot water and like it.
The enemy population, Mr. Gutzulesco stated, dees not
have enough clothing. Each person is given a ration of one
fiber suit a year. The fiber material is very poor and a
suit is usually worn out in about one month.
Production and Transportation. Mr. Gutsulesee had
little information regarding productien. He did net believe
that Japanese facteries were being moved to Manchuria. He
said that Japanese industry was to a considerable degree
dispersed in home industries.
Rail transportation, Mr. Gutzuleseo remarked, appeared
to be in fairly good condition. He believed that
communication lines would prove to be profitable air bembing
sargots.

passenger vessels. The samd was true of Fust
Motor vehicles, he said, were powered by a gatdtine-

Ceal and other fuels were short. Eleetric power was
strictly rationed in Tokyo.
Government. Mr. Gutzulesco's impression was that the
his opinion the present goternment could not attempt to

Korea. Mr. Gutzulesco passed through Korea without
any stopover and therefore was able to form only a flesting
impression. He stated that Korea struck him as being im
about the same condition as Japan Proper. In rospense to
an inquiry regarding traffic which he observed om his train
trip through Korea, Mr. Gutzulesco said that he say no
unusual movoment.
Manchuria. While he was in Manchuria during Jamuary
and Fobruary Mr. Gutzulesco gained the impresston that
conditions there were considerably bettor than in Japan
The Japanese in Manchuria had enough to eat. The Kwentang
army appeared to be well clothed, well fed and well
equipped. Rail traffic appeared to bé about normil.
The Chinese populetion was, Mr. Gutsulesoo obsorved
worse off than the Japanese. He said that the Chinese still
bitterly hated the Japanese. For the time being, hewever,
they were lying low and expecting that after the American
invasion of Japan the Soviet Union would ocoupy Manehurie.
Interestingly enough, Mr. Gutzulesco remarked that the
Chinese did not appear to believe that the Russians would
have to fight for Manchuria. His interpretation of
Manchurian-Chinese opinion was that following the American
conquest of Japan, Japanese contrel in Manchuria would
disintegrate, enabling the Russians to enter that territery
without meeting organised resistance. Mr. Gutsulesoo stated
that Chinese had remarked to him that they were waiting for
this development with pleasurable anticipation and were
looking forward to the liquidation of ali Japanese in
Manchuria.
It must be borne in mind, however, that the Chinese
to whom Mr. Gutzulesco talked were presumably those in
Harbin and Manchuli and were therefore more conscious of

The Cabinet Advisory system created by the ToJO Cabinet dealt
exclusively in war-time economy and did not transgress.this lime

perts and experienced men in such departments as aeroplanes,
shipping, finance, heavy industries and coal, were choeen as
advisors. The new aystem of Cabinet Advisors has removed such
limitations. These advisors have been made as political advi-
sors to the Premier. In government organisation, they have
likewise been stipulated "to participate in the comfidential
Thus, these advivore have been chosen widely from the ve-
rious phases of interior affairs, foreign affairs, culture and

one with the other. The system or supervisory commissars is to
be continued as it is, and the new system of advisore does not

it was the intention of the Prime Minister to preserve as many.

Speeding up the Ecforcenent of War-tine Policiee
The Composite Planning Board is a conaultory organisation to
the Premier in connection with the enforcement of policies. With
the abolition or the former "Planning Board", affairs related to
the General Mobilization Law were transferred to be hendled by
the Ministry of Military Supplies. The composition and adjuet-
ment of policies were brought under the care of the Cabinet in
which an office of Cabinet Councillors was inaugurated. The
present Composite Planning Board reflects such history of the
past, that is to say, it is something walking in the middle be-
tween the former "Planning Board" and the Office of Cabinet
Councillors. Compared with the Cabinet chief secretary, who
occupies the post as the number one foreman of the Cabinet taking
charge of political affairs, the Director of the Composite Plan-
ning Board occupies the post of the so-called chief of staff in
connection with the speeding up of the enforcement of war time
policies. The function of the Board as a consultory organization
to the Prime Minister deals with the coordination of enterprises
connected with important national policies, the adjustment of
affairs of the various ministries, and the composite consideratio
and investigation. By enterprises connected with important na-
tional policies are meant those connected with every phase of the
interal affairs with the exception of those connected with mili-
tary affairs and foreign diplomacy. By the adjustments of af-
fairs of the various ministries are meant the unitary composition
within the Cabinet of the much diversified affairs of the various
ministries at the present; while by the composite consideration
and investigation are meant those jobs such as the investigation
of administrative affairs, examination of past administration,
investigation of conditions of operation of the affairs of the
various bureaus, and the like, which are indispensable for making
perfect the penetration of the war-time administration. What is
worth our attention here is the trying out of the so-called
"test drilling" (演 東). This is the method whereby, in case
we don't know whether or not we will be able to reap best re-
sults if we put into practice a whole measure, then instead, we
just put one part of it into practice and try it out.
From the time of the start, the government hae been adopting
the principle that in the enforcement of policies the independent
originality and design of the various ministries are to be
counted upon to the utmost. This signifies the intention of the
Cabinet, from the Premier to the chief secretary of the Cabinet,
to avoid adopting the method of devising policies by themselves
and then asking the various ministries to have them materialized.
This intention of the Fremier passed on to the character of the
"Composite Planning Board" itself. As a planning board, it is

=== Page 112 === (confidence 0.97)
COORDNATED TRANSLATION CENTER
TR 300 - P. 5
CHUNGKJIG
quite matural that from its fnction it would devise and 
template pelicias. This does po, mean, however, that it will
confine itaelf w trin the reals of "contemplation" and will mst
seek to have the pclicies decided upon and put intc executiom.
Mcreover, it dos not meen tat he contemplated policies ar
the idea is nothing eise than to ccoperate with the varioue mi
nistries, to gtve perect life to the originality and design of
tre various -Inirtries and to speed up the enforcement of the
policies.
The mintenarce of an imeortant and substantial relationmi
with the Sup eme Var Directing Council may be taken as a great
character oi the Flanning Poard. The Supreme Council is not 
organizaticr from the stinipeint of the governmental organize-
tional system. On the cther hand the Flaining Board is an or-
ganization responsible directly to the Prme Minister in the
governmentel orgenization system. Accordingly there is no direet
relationship between the twe. Neverrelase as the national polf-
cies decided upon on the Supreme Council are brought back by the
Premier to the Cabinet where they are to be materlalized, so
naturally they will be touched by the hands of the Planning Board
which is the consultory orgenization to the Premier. On the
other hand, nat:onal policies which the Planning Board considere
as necessary are reflected on the Supreme Council by the Premier.
Under this sense, the plans are attributed with substantial re-
lationsh'p with the Supreme Council and the role they play in
this connection must be of importance.
Side by side with the Cabinet chief secretary, the President
of the Cabinet Information Bureau, and the Director of the Legis-
lation, the Director of the Composite Planning Board helps form
a circle surrounding the Premier. In a sense quite different
from that of the Cabinet ministers, he exercises role as the
"focus" of politics surrounding the Cabinet. Most of the Ca-
binet colleagues were advccatirg that the post should be held
concurrertly by the Chief Secretary TANAKA, nevertheless Mr.
HANIBA TETSUZO (* ), Vice-President of the CENTRAL
CHINA DEVELOPLENT COMPANY was eventually chosen for the job.
at the time when Premier KoIsO was the Minister and Chief Secre-
tary TANAkA was the Vice-Min'ster of Oversea Affairs. He is a
man who has entered the compeny of KOISO and TANAKA since long
ago. The functions of the Board are divided into the first
department (planning), the second department (readjusting) and
the third department (investigating), and are shared by fifteen
councillors. Besides, fifteen participants, and a number of
vice-participants and the like have been picked up from among
experienced and learned men of the people. Already MATSUDA
YOSHISUKE (公田令 ), Director of the Bureau of General
Affairs of the Ministry of Finance, has been appointed as Diree-
tor for the first department; ASAKA TOKUNARI (安 植 得 也),
Magistrate of ToChIG! Prefecture, has been appointed as Director
for the second department; and SUGANAMI YAJI(波 ,
Director of the Department of Coal of the Ministry of Military
Supplies, has been appointed Director for the third department.
At the same time a number of courcillcrs have been appointed.
Participants and vice-participants from among the people will be

The Politioal Congress (政 務 ) of the "Mongolian Govermment"

This year, the Government's main effort will be on the proseoution of the wr

The expenditures for this fisoal year have been estimated to run to
276, 750,000 yuen ( 元). Compared with the expenditures for the last fisoal
year, whioh were 147,979,000 yuen, the expenditures for this year will be
almost doubled. (The rest of the artiole is blurred.) The inoome from taxation
has inoreased 34.618, 000 yuen . . . The Government will issue high way Wonds

180,0c0,000 yuen.

the result that underground oities may even come into being. In previous
national defanse sohemes, underground faotories and cities suoh as these wre not

most essential subjeot for considerution today. Even in Germany whioh may be

only a very recent affair, i.e., after her reversas at Stalingrad and the Allies

Another instance of underground oity may be found in Mosoow where unden

The existance of underground factories and oities, and several-story under-
ground buildings is not only neoessary for tho maintainenoe and increase of
produotion at present, but will also be found useful in time to come.
Three important benefits stand out in the oonstruotion of underground
factories and cities. Firstly, the proteotion of ossential installations from air
raids as above mentioned can be realized. Secondly, viewed from the stratigioal
stand point military burdens for the protection of important oities and factorses

The Toohnique of Underground Factories
Whether in Berlin or in London, it seems that a certain gmount of
accomplishment has been made in the way of underground faotories. It is said t
that 5 to 15 meters will be considered safe as far as underground deptn is
oonoerned. However, with the development of tne explosive power in bombs, no
one oan be sure that these depths are absolutely free from danger. Therefore.
underground installations should be built with as thick a cover as possibls. In
underground work, not all places are suitable; investigations on the looality,
geology, and underground water table snould be first made to determine the
feasibility of the partioular location oonoerned. Moreover, favorable conditions
for housing the factory alone are not the only oriterion, as convenient and
spacious grounds will be needed for the construetion of aocessories such as living
quarters for staffs and workmen, offices, store-rooms, and transportation layosts.
In the case of the airoraft industry, in order to conoentrats a great many of its
subsidiary industries, specializing on individual parts, it is necessary to house
all related fagtories in the same belt underground which would give the appearanse
of a tunnel city. These elaborate plans should be fully considered in our main
oity and natiohai-defeapè plans, The urgent subjedt confronting us today is the
transfer of important workshops on surface to underground.

Aocording to FUJID, the relation between the tunael type of undergrousd
ing upon geologioal conditions and the ways of applioation, there will probehly
arise in practice many cases much more diffioult to deal with than those cites im
the table. FuIDA nas explained that the first kind indicated in the table is
applicable to the aocommdation of essential supplias or installations, and the
maximum length of a single opening should not be orar 50 meters, and in any ease
no longer than neoessary. To suit the requirments of storege or of the flow sbest
of operations, branch tunnels may be provided wnere necessary. The bramch tremele
may be laid out at right angles with the main oponings, or at two or more righs
angle turns as the nature of the operations may call for. In oase where right
angle bends are hard to make, protection walls with crooked passages may be befle
instead. Shield walls should be provided at all entrances and exits. Is cometswet
ed of consolidated earth, proteotion walls should have a thickness of l.2 moteres
in the case of earth bags, or tamped sand-filled partitions, they should be over
0.8 meters. Timber supports are necessary to guerd against the collapse of earth
embankments, both inside the tunnels and at the moutihs of openings. Side ditobee
should be dug to drain any underground spring water naturally into the open groued
outside. Moreover, ventilating shafts must be bored to afford natural ventilation
inside the tunnels. Where necessary, mechanical blowers for the partial or complet
regulation of underground air, and suitable means for the prevention of the forme
tion of dew may be adopted.
Installation of Underground Equipments:
As human beings are to stay and work underground, it bec mes essentiai that
the problens of ligit and air must be considered. The installation of artifioial
sun-light lamps is now possible and a certain structure in Tokyo today has already
been so equipped. with respect to ventilation, if vent holes are provided in the
ceiling and underground struotures, tunnels, and entrances are made to facilitate
the passing of air ourrents, not only will ventilation be accomplished through the
force of the wind, but natural ventilation may also be realized from the differenoe
in temperature between underground and surface airs. In addition, there are the
methods of ventilation by meohanical blowers and ozone generation. Water supply
and drainage problems underground are easily solved. In the event of attack from
poisonous gas, underground faotories can be comparatively easily protected through
air-tight arrangements at all openings communicating with the outside, while, on
the other hand, surface structures would te entirely vulnerable. For the transpor-
tation of supplies and products either into or out of the ground, inolines or oar
and passenger lifts can be oonstruoted without any diffioulty.
Aooording to UJIIE, expenditure for the engineering operations varies with
the type of factories. But, where as ordinary wooden structures having a minimm
safety faotor in strength cost from 600 to 700 yen per Tsubo ( 1 Tsubo equale
36 square feet, Japanese), underground tunnels canbe driven at a approximate cost
of 650 yen per Tsubo. The former requires much timber, while the latter uses lots
of oement.

oraft manufacture to the undergrcund will not be a easy task. Heavy industries.

the maohine industries: or the telegraphio, telephonic, and radar industry. the

industries that require low numidity cas also be installed underground. The only
drawback is that the workmen will beoome gloomy beoause of being barred from

ploughed fields or forests and the work of production is conducted silently day
or night, it goes without saying that the strength it represonts will indeed be
very great. This should be the futur trend for all faotories and cities.

THE INTERIAL PCLITICAL SITUATION OF JAPAN
By E. Zhukov.
In the course of the Puciric war substantial changes
have occurred. Anglo-American armed forces are under-
taking great offensive operutions on almost all fronts
of the Pacific theater of war. The Allies are moving
forard in Eurma. The troops of General IucArthur are
broadening their base of operations in the Philippine
Icl.ndr. Anerican B-29 long-range bombers are systema-
tically attackin: i utrial targets in Japan.
The activization of military operations in the Pacific
ocean and the approach of the war to the shores of the
Japanese islands has sn r e.ed t attention of inter-
national observers to the internal pclitical situation
of Japan.
The Japanese ress ivus little material upon which
to form an opilion on interual olitical shifts and moods.
Official infornation in Japan, even in the years of
relative flourishing of purliumentary policy, was
distinguished by its extraordinary one-sidedness and
incompleteness. Only by a collation of various indirect
indications is it possibl. to muke a general evaluation
of the direction and pers ctive of the political devel op-
ment of Japan.
In the sum ler of 1944 a chai u in the Japanese governg
ment took place. This event exerted a considerable influ-
ence on the political atmosphere in Japan. The forced
resignation of General Tojo, the recognized leader of
militaristic circles, showed that the idea of a military-
fascist dictatorsnip does not have support even in the
highest circles in Japan. General Tojo, whose government
in December l94l was the initiator of the unleashing of war
in the Pacific Ocean, in many respects copied the ill-fated
European fascist leaders. In the foreign press suggestions
were even made that Tojo would not mind playing the role of
a Japanese "fuehrer". There is no doubt that circles close
to General Tojo were really imbued with pro-Hitlerite "ideas".
All more or less independent people, known for their liberal
or simply moderate views, were mercilessly driven out of
the government apparatus. Those few officials who survived
that "purge" owed their survival exclusively to the fact
that they belonged to or were close to court circles.
Great

=== Page 134 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
Great hopes arose in parliamentary circles in Japan
in connection with the resignation of Tojo. The Parliament
in Japan formally exists and functions as it did before the
war. It wus not ablished or forbidden to function, but
the fascist "infection" poisoned in practice the applioation
of the Japanese constitution, which had existed unohanged
since 1889. During the years of the emergence into the
political lilelicnt of fascist officers and bureaucrats, the
relutive importance of the Japanese Parliament was greatly
decreused. The authority of the Parliament was always based
in the pust on its ability -- although to a limited degree --
to express public sentiment. The struggle of various
political urtics, Findin its reflection within the walls
or I rliuent, cre ed n impression f a curtain "independ-
ence" and an ability, sometimes at leust, to serve as a
soundin board for tne public opinion of the country.
In l940, parliamentary political parties in Japan were
dissolved. Any kind of criticism of the militaristio and
pro-fascist policy of Japanese cabinets was recognized as
inadmissible. An attempt wus made to unify the political
life of the country through the creation of a "united"
organization, able to absorb or subordinate to itself all
public and cultural organizations of the country. Such
an organization was the Taisei Iokusankai or "Association
for Aid to the Throne". The structure of the association
proved extraordinarily complicated and unwieldy. At its
head was placed the prime minister himself, and expenditures
for the mainteuance of the associution were included in the
state budget, whicn fron the very be inning gave it the
features of a clearly covermnent enterprise. Governors
headed local brunches of the association. The central
staff of the ssoci tion, rilled up with retired generals
and admiruls, wus overgrown with nunerous sections, bureaus,
committees and sub-comnittees. This cave to the new
organization a purely bureaucratic appearance. The absence
of a mass membership stren thened such an impression.
Fearing tne activity of the popular masses, not having
sufficient flexiuility to "condescend" to the level of the
ranks of the electors, the Association for Aid to the Throne
did not huve the capacity or the ability to replace the
former political purties. The leaders of the Association
soon proved to be generals without un army.
But Parliament, transformed into a voiceless gathering
of people deprived of the opportunity to criticize the
actions of the govern nent, ceased to play an active political
role.
The initiators of the creation of a "new political
structure"  as the beginning of a reorganization to a new
fascist order in Japan was called -- soon became convinoed
that the prohibition of political parties and the oreation
of

=== Page 135 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
of the unified Association for Aid to the Throne had not
achieved its purpcse. The "national unity" of which
Japanese reactionary circles dreamed had not been realized.
In particuler the eputies in Parliament grouped themselves
as fornerly accordin to the principle of prior allegiance
to this or that political party. The Association for Aid
to the Throne proved powerless to hinder this.
On May 15, 1942, a new organization, Iokusai Se1jika1
or "Political  soe tion or Ald to the Throne", was created.
It was to unite ull   ties of Parliament and all members of
the dissolveu politicul urties on the basis of a certain
compromise. Parlia. tay roups were ranted the facade of
organizational independence within the framework of allegiance
to a unified war program. The parliumentary "Political
Ass ciation for Aid to ths Throne" was declared formally
independent and enal in ri hts with the first, that is, the
bureaucratic Acsoci tion for Aid to the Throne, remaining
"outside politics". This showed thut even General Tojo
recognized the im cse t ility of realizin the original plan
of absolute fuscist unification of the whole political life
of Jupan.
The bankruptoy of the military-political course of
Tojo and the creation in  summer of 1944 of a new govern-
mental combination in which prominent representatives of
former parliamentary groups were inclu ied, revived the hopes
of leaders of the old bourgeois-lancowner parties of Japan
for the possibility of their full legulizatio. In distinotion
from its predecessor, the Tojo government, th: cubinet of
Koiso-Yonai cannot be called openly pro-fascist. Soon after
the coming to power of Koiso, the Japanese press began to
discuss plans for the "re-creation" of parliamentary parties,
and the new cabinet of Koiso-Yonai made a series of gestures
which were interpreted as promises alol this line. The
sphere of the Associution for Aid to th T rone was narrowed.
The new government refused to give t sunport to this
unpopular, un-unified party that it  ad forly enjoyed.
General Abe resigned from the post of Pre ident of the
Political Association for Aid to the Throne a d was succeeded
by Admiral Kolayashi. (In Japan & trudition has become
established accordin to which naval men re supposed to be
distinguished by gre ter "liberalism" than army men. Thus,
this shift wis interpreted as a sin of  favourable attitude
toward parliamentary circles). This impression was further
strengthened after the recent d-signution of Kobayashi -- as
the representative of the Parliamenturians -- to the post of
Minister without Portfolio. In this connection, the crisis
of such a clearly semi-fascist organization as the "Union of
Youth" of Japan was a  ravated. The tendency of the leaders
of the organization -- prominent figures of the rightist camp,
the

=== Page 136 === (confidence 0.97)
the rutired General Tatekawa and the retired Colonel
Hashimoto, -- to attain autonomy of this organization and
to rant to it special rignts transforming it not only into
a ur reserve, but to un independent channel of ideological-

failed. This failure uguin evidenced the lowering of the
influeuce oi prc-Iitlerite forces in Japan. Parliamentary

arp protest from some Japanese military ciroles. But the

show that the influence of perliamentary ciroles has some-
What increased. The new ohief of the "Union of Youth" is
a "civilian" -- the chairman of the Japanese Information

The course of the Paciic war undoubtedly is strengthen-
in the disarpointment of the Japanese people in the polioy

 l saly become "a field or battle" and may meet with
etill worse rials. The Japanese government introduced for

 i difficult problens put forward at the session in the

Japan has waged war for a long time. The Japanese
rulin classes in l93l were the first to start on the road
of violent repartition of the world after the first world
war. But down to l94l the aggresive actions of Japan with
few exceptions did not require too much effort on her part.
The saorifices made by Japan during the time from the autumn
of l93l until the moment of the coming of the crisis in the
course of the Pacific War, that is until the beginning of
1944, were comparatively small. Under these conditions the
Japanese man in the street was much more easily subjeot to
military pro-fasoist propaganda which had inculoated faith
in the military invincibllity of Japan and in the absolute

recently did Japan gradually begin to know the bitterness

of defeat. Now, this feelin of weakness before super ior

the frequent complaints of the Japanese press about all

The logic of the development of military events and
in articular the economic consequences of the prolonged
war are reflected in the attitudes of the population. In
spite of she hith degree of mobilization of Japanese
industry for war needs, fuctories are not coping with
growing war orders. The recent dismissal of the Japanese

Fujiwara, and his replacenent by Yosida, a representat ive
of bureaucratic circles, aroused a certain perplexity and
oven agitation amon a certain part of the Japanese bourgeoi-

Japan always imported a c nsiderable part of her food
requirements. Under war conditions this import has proved
very jirficult, not only because of the sharp decrease in
means of transport (chierly shipping) but because of the
approach of military operations on the seu and in the air
to the Japunese islunds.
At the beginnins of January 1o 45, the Japanese news-

(75 grammes per week)  the almost complete absence of

=== Page 157 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Division of Economie Security Control
Political Biographic Section
May 13, 1945
To: FC - Mr. Vougyear
From: Hendrik van Oss
Subject: Translation of Japanese Who's Who
I am enclosing a copy of an "International Who's
Who" of Japanese liberals presented to this Division
by Mr. Dooman, FE. According to Mr. Dooman, the book
contains in large part biographic information on Japanese
left wing individuals, although it includes as a cloak,
information on other prominent Japanese persons, Europeans
and Americans. Mr. Dooman feels that the book has impor-
tant possibilities and may present information on names
which will be very useful to this Government in its oper-
ations in Japan.. Mr. Dooman requests that a high security
classification be given to this work.
I should appreciate your presenting this to the
appropriate section of the War Department. If it is
possible for the translation to be done in the War
Department, it would be well to arrange for a specific
number of copies for the Department's use. Any distri-
bution outside the War Department or the Department of
State should be cleared through Mr. Dooman. The comments
concerning non-Japanese, or concerning non-political figures
(1.e., athletes, actors, etc.) need not be translated.

MIMORANDUM
RESTRICTED
by a uhinese student recently returned 
wuntry.
Bationing: When tue informant left Japan, in October, 1944,
foodstuffs were scarce and were subject to strict rationing. vivil-
ians were dividea into turee categories for purposes of rationing.
The basic rice ration was 273 grammes per person; a manual laborer,
nowever, received 50x more than this basic ration. In the oourse
of a month the average personm al so got a few ounces of meat and
rish and a limitea quantity of vegetaules.
Rationing of clotning, on a point basis, was also in offoot.
Until last year a man was ailowed lo0 points a year for clothing,
but last year this was cut to firty points for a man under rifty
years of age and to forty points for older men. in orter to en-
courage marriages, an additional loo points were allowed couples
getting married. The informant states that fifteen points were
needed in order to buy a snirt and sixty points for a suit of
clothes.
Diet ana rublic Health: Rice on the market is  unpolished,
has mixed with it 10% of corn (or, in winter, of potatoes) and
is not easily digested by the Japanese. such flour as is avail-
able is very dark. seafood is not plentiiul, and many persons
become ill as a result of eating spoiled fiah. Vegetables amil-
able on city markets are insufficient in quantity--thus one ououm-
ber might be the only vegetable a city family of three might get
for a three-day period. ▲ very little sugar was still available
--perhaps half a cup per person each month.
As a result of inadequate diet, night blindness had become
common. The Chinese students in Japan found the amount of food
allowed insufficient for thenselves and surdoed that the health
of the Japanese must be sufferring.
Raw Material Shortages: When the informant left Japan, raw
material shortages were numerous. The materials which were short
included leather, cotton, wool, salt, matohes, soap, paper, motals,
rubber, oil and coal. as a result of the leather ahortage, no
leather shoes had been available to civilians for several years;
small pieces of leather could still be purchased for reinforcing
the bottoms of shoes or for stitching together to make belts.
No cotton goods (excepting cotton substitutes) and but a few poor
woolens were on the market. wetals have disappeared from the
Japanese soene with a ocompleteness which is probably almost un-
rivalled: metal objects have disappeared from shops, have beon
surrendered by households, and have been removed wherever found-
clothing hooks and hatracks from trains, the meohanims of barber
chairs, metal bells everywhere and tne metal of doors and windows.
The rubber x shortage is interesting; at rirst the Jap-
anese believed that they woula have more rubber than they ocould
use as such and they accordingly converted some of it into liquid
fuels and then later tney found they could not transport it from
malaya in the expected quantities and they began to make artirioial
rubber. Frobably the most serious shortages are thess of eil end

=== Page 168 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
coal. Thus the shortage of fuel oil prevents the full use of
fishing boats whioh might bring in much needed foodstuffs. In
order to save gasoline all civilian motor vehiales have seam
converted to the much less efrici ent burning of charooal; due
to lack of adequate transport some fruits have rotted in the
countryside. The shortage of coal limits the amount of aleetrie
power whioh can be generated therefrom, and this is partioularly
serious in the dry season, when less waterpower is available.
(The informant had not heard reports, very prevalent in uhina,
of the Japanese storing coal under water along the Japanese .)
The coal shortage has al so led to a shortage of produots requiring
substantial amounts of coal in their manufacture--suoh as glass
and chinaware. Thus some shortages lead to other shortages, exert-
ing a paralyzing influence on the nations life.
Taxation and sale of National Loan Certificates: Tne infor-
mant Is not able to present any but an incomplete picture of Jap-
anese taxation. ne says, however, that taxation of various mrts
there is high. Thus cigarettes which formerly sold for ten 
a package now cost sixty sen, the added fifty sen representing
added taxation. There is an entertainment tax of lo0% on theater
tickets and similar items and a 200% tax is levied on luxury
itms.
A substantial proportion of salaries is deducted for the
compulsory purchase of national loan certificates--bonds due
perhaps three years after the date of issue. The informant says
the family in the home of whicn he roomed had a monthly inoome of
a little less than 2oo yen from which sixty yen were deduoted
each month for such purposes. The certificates are on sale every-
where--in stores, in railway stations, etc.
(It was stated by the informant that when he left Japan
late last year the price level was avout 80% above that of
September 18, 193l--most of the rise having occurred since
rearl Harbor.)
Some Apparent ffects of the war on the Life of civilians:
The effect of abortageaar foodstuffs has been noted above. The
war, as al so noted el sewhere in this report, has also resulted
in the closing of sonools above the primary grade. (The infor-
mant, incidentally, describes Japanese s tudents as generally
reluctant to enter the armed services.) Klevators, because of
a power ahortage, have stopped running. pecause of this and
in view of a scarcity of consumer goods, department stores have
moved romaining goods to the lower floors and have closed ofr
the upper floors. rewer newspapers with fewer and maller edi-
tions are being published(because of a shortage of paper).
There also is less light to read by, electric current being
rationed; accordingly most people, having nothing better to do,
go to bed early. Travel is restricted: when one wishes to make
a trip he must apply to the police station in his precinct for
a permit to buy a railway ticket.
mployees in factories now are largely women. some women having
spuses xxx or fiancees in the armed forces, the informant alleges,
are having illicit relations with men remaining behind. Several
years ago, he says, a law was passed--in an attempt to keep up
the birth-rate, perhaps--attempting to place illegitimate child ren
on a basis of legal equality with other children (an illegitimate
child now mk may take its mother's surname and may inherit prop-
erty from her).
People in the cities were, when the informant left Japan,
worried about air-raids. rire-lanes had been construoted and
many slit-trenohes (but few air-raid shelters) had been dug.

=== Page 169 === (confidence 0.97)
-3-
The military appear still to be optimistic about the mr but
some civilians (and especially the members of the wealthier mer-
chant class) have become pessimistic and some or them expeet a
large-soale invasion of the main ialands. (Two Kuomintang under-
ground workers from north uhina say the Japanese there are eem-
paring the present with the period of uhina's Yuan dynasty whe
the Japanese faced the threat of a mongol invasion, that loeal
officials in that country now command militia units made up of
local residents not in the regular forces and that the Japanese
are talking of village-to-village resistance in case of invadon.
One of these informants says he not long ago read an artiole by a
Japanese newspaperman named 川 wno wrote mtrdgxkis advising the
people to build airplanes and yet more airplanes and deseribing
Japanese solaiers he nad witnessed in the rhilippine lalands as
each night looking in vain for the arrival of warplanes from the
homeland.)
Destruction of mulberry Groves: Almost all mulberry groves
in Japan have been out down in order to provide additional land
for growing rice and other foodstuffs. (Silk could still oe
purchased when the informant left Japan, so the Japanese assumably
have adequate supplies of tuis material for military uses.)
Treatment of seriously wounded soldiers: I have heard perais-
tent reports that Japanese soldiers in china commondly are killed
by their officers or medical men if they are so serioualy wounded
that it appears they would not oe able to recover surriciently to
again become military efrectives. 1 therefore asked the informant
whetner ue had seen any crippled soluiers in Japan. ne ssid he
nad soen an exhivition or about loo who had lost legs and haa
been provided witn metal limbs. when he saw them they were all
bicycling through the streets together, aemonstrating to the publio
the emtent to which they were able to function like other per sons.
ne al so occasionally saw soluiers, who haa lost an arm or a leg in
puvlic--but he says he understands they are enployed in factories
and do not generally appear on the streets. Those he saw were
still in uniform and wore armbanas witn red crosses on them.
mployment of rrisoner-or-war Labor: The informant says he
saw a foreign prisoner wiom he took to be an american soldier
working as a baggage coolie at the rokyo central station. ne
knew the prisoner to ve englisu-speaking vecause ne near him
saying, "Excuse me" wnile passing taroueh tne crowds. A Japnese
enuarme at the station vas seeping watcn on ihe prisoner.
Accoraing to press reports, wilitary prisoners were being
useu as farn laborers in lne Japanese countrysiue.
war and ducntion in Japan: mhen the inrormant lert Japan
almost everyvody vetween the ages o1 lo anu 40, except for sone

or law, political science, economics anu similar studies (斗, or

Return Trip from Javan: The informant went from Shimomsei

July 24,
U - Mr. Grew
JAPAN
Politioal Association to Hear Report on Emperor's
Health and on Foreign Affairs. A Tokyo broadoast of
July 24 in Japanese to Greater East Asia reports that
"in order to set the present mental attitude of the
894.00/7-2445
government and the people for a decisive war" the Great
Japan Political Association will meet tomorrow morning
at the Diet Building in Tokyo.
Scheduled on the agenda will be "a talk on the latest
state of His Majesty's health" by Dr. Shimomura, President
of the Board of Information, and a report "on the details
of the international situation" with an explanation of
the "government's foreign policy" by Foreign Minister Togo.
Comment: While this will probably be a meeting to
bolster morale against possible serious developments at
the Potsdam conference, with advance notice of the im-
portant topics on the agenda given to draw popular atten-
tion, another possibility seems worth bearing in mind.
Shouid the Japanese be preparing to surrender, the frail
state (whether real or feigned) of the emperor's health
would be about the only excuse which could be advanoed to
end resistance with convincing "saving of face".

m
wholly unsubstantiated rumors from Europe allegedly based
on information from Tokyo that an attack had been made on
his life. On the other hand, Radio Tokyo has reported
that within the last ten days he has held six audienoes
hl
with Premier Suzuk1, Foreign Minister Togo and Munitions
