Enalcaure to Deapatch No. 4. dated Octeber 10.
on the subject "General Plan for maformation of
108
MEMORANDUM FOR: Supreme Commander and Chief of StafY.
SUBJECT: Reformation of Japanese Government.
One of the obvious difficulties in the way of a suitable
reformetion of the Japenese Government is the reactionary atsitude,
due to early training and long bureaucratic service, of the sub-
ordinate officials of the various ministries It is to be assmed.
for example, thet most officials in subordinete (but importent)
executive posts have served from fiffteen to twenty-five years.
at least the last fourteen of which have been devoted to the further-
Ence of Jepanese eggression end imperielism, and there is reason
to believe that Cabinet ministers and vice ministers who honestly
wish to institute reform feel themselves blocked by the reactionary
attitudes or passive resistance of such subordinetes.
By rough estimete the recent directive of October 4, 1945 for
the removel of restriction on politicel, civil end religious
liberties should cruse the abolition of fifty percent of the pesitions
in end under the Home Ministry. As those remaining will doubtlessly
be for the most pert reactionary old hands and as reportedly many
in office during the war who were responsible for resressive, pelicies
that in due course efter the directive has been carried out the
Ministry be required to replace fifty percent of the remmimins pom-
sonnel with new personnel. It is also recommended that a simil
procedure be adopted with regard to other ministries so that withim
e few months time seventy-five percent of the old persommel will be
replaced by new. The Justice Ministry, for example, should 
thoroughly overhauled, especihlly the procurator's departmept. As
regerds the Ministry of Finance, much in this directism has probably
been accomplished by the shutting down of imperialistic banking in-
stitutions and the process could be carried through the gorermment
monopolies (tobacco, salt, camphor) and also through the gqrermment-
controlled industrial associations. As for the Ministry of Wer and
Navy, it is believed that they should be entirely abolished when
demobilization end surrender of oquipment and facilities have been
accomplished as their contimued existence thereafter would be with-
out justification in e demilitarized country.
If some such plan is adopted, it is further recommended that
eppropriate officials of the Japanese Government be so informed in
order thet they may have opportunity themselves to take the desired
action.
GEORGE ATCHESON,JR.
George Atcheson, Jr.

This despatch from Tokyo discusses
political parties in Japan.
The follow-
ing is a thumh-nail resume:
From the beginning of the China
Incident in 1937 the o1d line parties
declined in influence, and were dis-
solved 1n 1940. Thereafter, attempts
were made to form a kind of national
totalitarian party, but without much
real success.
Defeat presents Japan for the first
time with conditions under which a genuine
party forn of rovernment may evolve.
Whether and when sueh a government may
emerge is unpredictable.
There are only three political
groups which have any inportance at
present:
(1) the "Liberal Party", under the
leadership of atoyama, an old-line
politician. This party is actually
more conservative than liberal.

Sir:
I have the honor to report that in a memorandum dated
October 3, 1945 from the Chief of Staff, it is requested
that this office undertake "a study of the politioal parties
of Japan, their background, personalities and objeetives,
with a special purpose of determining their trends." Copies
of this memorandun and of a menorandum on the subjeot of
political parties in Japan, dated October 11, 1945, which
was forwarted to the Supreme Commander and the Chief of
Staff, are enclosed. The memorandum represents only an
initial and prelininary survey of this important aspeot of
Japanese political activity.
Summary of Memorandun: In and around Tokyo, thore are
many political groups forming, but only three at the moment
show definite signs of national importance: the "Liberal
Party," the socialists, and the communists. The Liberal Par-
ty is under the Ieadership of Tchiro H ToYtl, former exeou-
tive director of the defunct Seiyukai party and professional
politician and Diet menber for many years. The party, com-
posed for the most part of professional politioians with a
few journalists, diplomats, and colloge professors, is more

COPY
GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
UNITED STATES ARMY FORCES, PACIFIC
OFFICE OF THE CHIEF-OF-STAFF
3 October 1945
MEMORANDUM:
FOR
Mr. Atcheson.
The Supreme Commander requests that you undertake
a study of the political parties of Japan, their background,
personalities and objectives, with a special purpose of
determining their trends. Your studies and advice will be
requested in the consideration of the development of the
political structure in the modification of the feudal and
authoritarian tendencies and the development of a peace-
fully inclined and responsible goverrment. Your studies
will contribute largely to the determination of the per-
sons who will be permitted to participate in government,
in insuring that membership is held only by persons who
may be relied upon to further the purposes of the occupa-
tion.
The Supreme Commander requests that you give him weekly
a memorandum setting forth the results of your studies and
any recommendations you may desire to present in the pre-
mises.
R. K. SUTHERLAND
R. K. Sutherland,
Lieutenant General, U. S. Army,
Chief of Śtaff.

OFFICE OF THE UNITED STATES POLITICAL ADVISER
Tokyo, Japan,
UNCLASSIPTED
October'11, 1945.
MEMORANDU. FOR: Supreme Commander and Chief of Staff.
SUBJECT: Political Parties and Political Movements in Japan.
In compliance with the request in your memorandum of
October 3, 1945, we have undertaken a study of the above
subject, with the special purpose of determing the tronds
of the parties in question. We have requested that the
Department of State furnish us such pertinent biographic
information as may be available from the various Govern-
mental agencies in Washington. Locally we have asked for
the cooperation of G-2 and CIC to the extent that we may
receive, upon request, information on file in those sections
regarding the political activities and beliefs of individual
Japanese whose names we may come upon in connection with
our investigation and whose history and background may be
unknown to us.
There is enclosed the first report on the general sub-
ject. This report is necessarily preliminary in nature. We
shall expect to supplement it with further and more com-
prehensive reports to be forwarded to you weekly.
GEORGE ATCHESON, JR.
George Atcheson, Jr.

Party Program
The Socialist Party. in eddition to its policies on economic and
fimancial matters previously annoumoed, has now presented a platferm
outliming its stend on lmbor, agrioulture, social affairs, womem's
affairs, and culture. The Party has also proposed a prepertismal
representatiem electoral system and specific refoms in the smpervisiem
of campaigms and elections.

=== Page 248 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
The Party advocates establishment of a Labor Ministry, recogmition
of labor wmioms, minimm wage laws, the 48 hour week and measures to
insure employment. Its proposals for agrarian reform are still rather
vague. There is demand for "fundamental revision of the present system
relative to the treatment of arable land", for mechanisatiom of agri-
culture, state comtrol of fertilisers, and organisation of farmers' and
fishermen's cooperatives. Social insurance is proposed as well as
relief measures for war wonnded, bereaved families, and air-raid victims.
speedy housing construction, expansion of the radio industry and dis-
tribution of radio sets. The principle of equal rights for wemen is
upheld, including exrtension of equal employment rights to mem and wemem,
dissemination of political education among women, and the abolitiom of
prostitution. In the field of culture and education, the Party adreeates
encouragement of scientific research, adoption of the metric system, ex-
tension of the period of compulsory education, removal of State restrist-
ion on freedom of religion, and encouregement of the adoption of
Esperanto as an international lenguage.
Popular Front Proposal
On October 19 Yoshio SHIGA, one of the leaders of the Japenese
Communist Party recently released from Fuchu Prison, approached leaders
of the Socialist Party with a proposal of collaboration to establish a
Populer Front. After discussion between Shiga and several Socialist
leaders, including Chosaburo MIZUTANI, Rikiso HIRANO, and Mosaburo
SUZUkI, the Socialists decided against formal collaboration. It was
announced that since the Communist platform had not yet been mede public.
it was too early for the Socielists to enter a popular front movement.
However, they expressed the desire to cooperate after both parties were
well established.
Opinion on the question of a united front with the Commmists is
divided within the Socialist ranks. Quite a few are reported to favor
joining the Communists, including some of the lesser known members. mong
these are said to be Hideo YAMAHANA and Minoru TAKANO,
The two issues which make it difficult for the Socialists and
Communists to reach en agreement are the Emperor question and the question
of confiscation of private property. The Socialist Party is far from umited
on these questions. At the same time there have already appeared evidences
of disunity on these issues emong the Communists themselves. Some Cemmeumists
take a line similar to that of the Tenan (North China) group, holding that
direct attack on the Emperor system will alienate supporters, and that it
is first necessary to educate the people politically. On the other hand.
Kyuichi TOKUDA and Yoshio SHIGA, who immediately declared themselves
opposed to the Emperor system kpomrtheir release from prison, present the
view that unless the system is eredicated, it will be revived and will
again become dangerous when Allisù occupation ends or in the event of war
between the United States and Soviet Russia.
Certain observers are of the opinion that if a Popular Front develops,
it will begin in local districts. The first evidence is the formation of
a group in Nagano Prefecture, calling themselves the Ina Peoples Party
(Ina Jimmin-to). This organization inaludes Communists, Socialists,
members of the Farmers Youth Union and other local agrarian groups. Also
in Yameneshi Prefeeture both socialist and comesanist elements are reported
to be active. From such local movements there may srise the demand for a
Popular Fromt on a natiomal scale.

-5-
Conclusiome
At present it still appears thet the Socialist Party mey be seeend
in strength to the Liberal Party of Ichiro HaroYAmA. However. if it om
avoid en open break among its diverse elements, it stands to gain widely
in populer support. It still lecks an outstanding leader. Some cireles
bel ieve thet if Ikuo OYAA were to return from Chicago. he would
immediately be accepted as head of the party. The Japamese press has
featured interviews which he has given to Americen correspomdemts and his
reputation in Jepan is apperently high. He has the great advamtage of
heving been outside of the country during the entire period of Japem's
military aggression.

By Kyuichi TokUDh, Yoshio SiiIGs, and the others of the JapN
CJMMUNI T ARTY ho were released from imprisonment.
1. We express profound gretitude that the first step towards
a demoorstio revolution in Japan has been takon through the

2. In respeot of the peace polioies of America and Britaim

tarougn the overthrow of the Imperial Institution.
The tyrennioel oppression of the military polioe type
besed upon e long-stending feudalistic ideology, the eruel

deoeptive nature.

and even the establishment of world peace, may go up in empty
bubble and foam.
6. Suoh a mission cannot be accomplished under the leader
ship of those supporters of the Imperial institution whe we-
present the false liberalism and false socialism that flou-
rished by continual compromises with the authority of the
Imperial system under feudalistic despotism. Together with
the Imperial system, these men have lost the confidemee of
the peopl by using deception as a weapon of self-preserva-
tion. Moreover, they can wield nothing whatever that eould
possibly commend them to the confidenoe of the natioms of
the world.
7. It is we, the truly demooratio political offenders whe
have been freed herewith, who are the very ones to bear this
tremendous mission with the masses of the people, Moreover,
oreating a united front with all the organizations and ia-
fluenoes that have these objectives in common with us, a
peaceful government of the people may be construeted on that
foundation. And with a selfless spisit of sacrifiee, ex-
peoting no rewards whatever, we look forward to making pro-
gress in carrying out our responsibilities.
Translated by Paul H. Bumida

3. Information from country districts is inadequate
but indicates they are generally still unaffcoted by

cracy, food control officials, etc. Reoent heavy damage
by rain to crops in many areas may bring nearer time when
discontent will takc more tangible form. There are yet
no signs of organization or political activity by returned
soldiers but Socialists and reactionary alliance of
laborers have made program bid for their support and
other parties will certainly follow.

4. Old labor unions, particularly in trenepest,

Bulk of population, however, is still apethetic and

seriousness of coming economic distress and this is beins

5. Constitutional reform has become subject of
active discussion. Although there 1s some support for
view (held by Shidehara, Minobe and others) that present
constitution can be interpreted flexibly, agreement is
fairly general that revision is desirable. There eppears
to have been some rivalry between the Cabinet and the
Konoye-Kido group of the imperial household and Knoye has
been bitterly attacked in the press for statements
allegedly made to the Associated Press intimating that
he had been charged with drawing up a draft and for
suggesting that the Emperor might abdicate. Public
opinion has opposed "secret" revision without opportumity
for publio discussion but has desired the retention, to
a fairly high degree, of the imperial institution.
6. Opinion regarding war responsibility has become
more specific. After criticism of Konoye, for reasons
mentioned above and because of his role as leader before
the Pacific war, he announced that he desired to
relinquish his title. There have been suggestions that
other peers should follow suit. The staffs of the main
newspapers have demanded resignations of direotors on
charge of war responsibility.
Asahi complied (although it is not indicated that
there has been change in ownership) but Yomiuri refused
and the paper has in effect been seized and is being
published by junior editorial ataff and printers.
Criticism of old Diet as a group sharing war responsibility
has resulted in about ten resignations.

SBORET
-2- #107, November 9,from Tokyo vie War
Fearing criticiam of inaction, Cabinet set up
War Calamities Rehabilitation Board", under Ichizo
Kobayashi who was made Minister without Portfollio, 
with statement that it would plan rebuilding of des-
troyed cities and provision of housing. General
estimate of probable effectiveness was not optimistic.
Conference of prefecturel governors, first since
surrender, was given "explanation of Cabinet's
administrative policies and measures to cope with
economics situation." Administrative Adjustment
Council under cabinet was announced to study re-
duction of govt personnel by one half. Considerable
importance seemed attached by Cibinet to body being
set up to study the war, with publicity emphasis on
"why war vas lost". An indication of presently sub-
merged tensions was an unsuccessful and unexplained
ettompt on life of the Homo Minister. Several Cabinet
Ministers showed their ties with the past by making
the traditional pilgrimage to Ise and Emperor vas
cheduled to visit the shrine to report end of war.
POLITICS. Cabinet
SECRET

=== Page 267 === (confidence 0.97)
stoR
-3- #107, Novomber 9, from Tokyo via War
POLITICS.
Cabinet announcement that it was considering
"restrictive plural voting system" provoked criticism
by Liberal parties who, apparently fearing inebility
to win majorities, favored straight proportional
representation and claimed that proposal would
play into hands of experienced political manipulators.
Voters would select one name in districts having less
than six representatives, two in districts having six
to ten, three where more than ten. Cabinet's choice
of Parliamentary Vice Ministers was widely attacked
on grounds some had been members of War Cabinets and
as anachronism. Imperial rescript called diet session
for December 24 but explanation was that Cabinet would
ask for special session early in December to pass
election law and then dissolve. Diet generally was
sporadically criticized as reactionary and sharing
in war guilt. Konoye affair died down with the Prince
losing some face after statement by General MacArthur.
Constitutional reform received less public attention
although an
SECRET

=== Page 268 === (confidence 0.99)
SLORET
-8- #107, November 9, from Tokyo via War
although an "Independent civilian group" to study
subject announced itself.
PARTIES.
The Socialist Party, at present the most promising
new group, held formal inauguration meeting, passed
a broad program (by methods open to democratic criticism)
adopted an official name (Socialist in Japanese, Social
Democratic in English), and chose slate of middle of
the road leaders, presumably in hope of uniting partyb
many factions. At least one instance reported of
local cooperation between Socialist and Liberal
parties. Minor parties proliferated, most appearing
to have reactionary backing (such as one supported by
Nakajima the aircraft producer) and to be offshoots of
former Nippon Seijikai. Large funds pcssibly available
to some of these parties make them unpredictable fictors.
Communist Party was expanding organization and interest-
ing itself in labcr controversies but actual strength
remained unknowm and probably not large.
LABOR
Considerable activity in labor organization,
including fishing

Enclosure to Despatol. No.:50 of November 13, 1945 from
the Acting United States Political Adviser to the Sup-
reme Commander for the Allied Powers, Tokyo, Japan, om
the subject "Views of Japanese Labor Economist (Tatsuo)
MORZTO) on Constitutional Roform and other Current Je-
panese Problems".
MEMORANDUM OF CONVERSATION
November 7, 1945.
Participants: Tatsuo MCRITO, Staff Member of OHARA
Social I'roblems Research Institute
r. John X. Emmerson
Subject: Constitutionel Reform and other Current
Japanese Iroblems.
Mr. Morito, whose biography is appended, callod at
my office on November 7, 1945. Mr. Morito is an impor-
tant member of the staff of the OHARA Social Problems
Research Institute. After some discussion of the his-
tory and present status of that well-known organization
(See Appendix III), I turned the conversation to an ex-
ploration of Mr. Morito's views on current Japanese pro-
blems. The following is & sunmary of his remarks on the
subjects indicated.
Constitutional Revision
(a convenient opening was provided by
the recent newspaper announcement that
e group of scholars, including Profes-
sor TAlANC and .r. Morito, both of the
CHARi Institute, were organizing to
make an independent study of the revi-
sion of the Constitution.)
One of the tesks of the Japanese Cultural Federa-
tion which is to be formally organized on November 13
will be to study revision of the Constitution. The Cul-
tural Federetion has already set up three departments:
(1) Sociel Sciences; (2) Natural Sciences; (3) Art and
Literature. A special comnittee of the Social Sciences
Section will undertake e study of Constitutional reform.
Both the Cabinet and the Office of the Lord Privy
Seal are now giving attention to the problem of revision
of the Constitution. However, this is not sufficient.
It is imperative that the people themselves consider ser-
iously this problem. So far the government has called
only upon Constitutional lewyers to assist in this work.
Lawyers however are technicians and may not adequately
represent the politicel and sociel views of the poople.
For example some of these scholers have already stated
that the presert Constitution is flexible and does not
need revision.
The most important result to be achieved by Consti-
tutional reform is protection of the peoples' rights.
The present Constitution affords protection to private
property but ignores the acknowledged rights of the people.
The Diet system must be revised to make the House of Peers
a body

=== Page 280 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
a body representing professions rather than priviloged
classes.
The Cabinet must be made responsible to the represen-
tative legislative body. The political and religious funo-
tions of the Emperor must be seperated. The peerpie rights
must be protected and the Emperor must become no more than
a moral symbol. His position must be similar to that of
the British and Scandinavian Kings. In fact the Emperor
himself hopes thet his position will be thus demooratized.
The feeling of respect toward the Imperial House is
still strong in Japan especially in rural districts. This
was proved by the way the war ended. Therefore the Empe-
ror system and the Imperial Household cannot be suddenly
abolished without creating greet confusion within the ooun-
try. The revised Constitution must prevent the use of the
Emperor by powerful groups. Sovereignty should rest nomi-
nally in the Emperor but actually in the people. The po-
wer of the Emperor should be controlled by the Constitution
and he should be able to act only with consent of the Diot.
He should not have any veto power.
Those close to the Throne want to protect it more oven
than the Emperor himself, who actually has democratic ideas.
Marquis KIDo cannot avoic moral responsibility for the war.
He will undoubtedly resign his position in the near future.
University Reform
Lr. Iwasaburo Trno has proposed the following reforms
in Tokyo Imperiel University:
a. Retirement of the president.
b. Chenge or the name of "Imperial" University.
c .
Reinst: te:ent of professors who have been
dismissed because of their political beliefs.
The following ere examples:
OUCHI,
TSUCHIYA,
WAL IMUhA,
ARISAWA,
KIMUR.,
YANATHRA,
Fumio YAMADA,
Seitaro AMAlA, end
Tatsuo MORITO
d.
Reform Economics Department.
(Recent press notices indicate that most
of the above reforms are actually being
carried out at the present time.)
Politicel Parties
The Liberal Party is made up for the most part of Diet
members and does not have great strength at the present
time

=== Page 281 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
time. Under the prevailing conaitions of potential unrest
and unemployment, it is likely that extremists, inoluding
both Fascist and Communist elements, may inorease rapidly.
One of the serious defects in the new Sooial Domooratio
Party is a predominance within its ranks of old politioians.
There are few young nen and few intellectuals. Those Labor
and Socialist leaders who are now coming to the front have
been forced into inactivity during the past ten years and
leck mental and physical vigor. In spite of the new freedom
and political rights accorded to wonen, none were in atton-
dance at the recent Socialist rally in Hibiya Hall.
Trade unions are very weak at the present time. With
the disappearance of heavy industry the most influential
labor groups are disbanded. Labor is best organized now
in the transportation and communications industries.
The farmers generally bettered their situation during
the war along with the militerists, leading Bureaucrats
and war industrialists. Peasants are generally conservative
and the strength of agrarian movements is doubtful at the
present time.
The former Japanese Political Association controls
the majority of seats in the present Diet. It has strong
financial backing.
The principel policy differences between the Liberal
and Socialist Parties are their economic and finencial pro-
grans. The Soci:lists advocate state planning and national-
ism of meny enterprises wherees the Liberals propose free
economic activity. The Libersls advocate return of the
rsilroads to private menegement.
Future Development of Japan
It is a nistake to believe that Japan should devolop
an exclusive agrtrien economy. It is importent for Japan
that industry be permitted to revive. The industrial work-
ers ere the backbone of Democ racy while the farmers are
reactionary. The ide& of revenge renains strong among the
peasants. Therefore, industry is essentiel not only from
the point of view of the economic rehebilitation of Japan
but also for the growth of Democrecy.
If Japan is to develop Democracy, the positive impetus
must come from the Japenese people. The influence of Alliod
Headquerters will be principally negative, thet is to sup-
press militaristic and netionalistic elements. Cultural edu-
cetion is imperative especially for the farmers. A poli-
ticel, cultural and social revolution is absolutely neces-
sary if Japan is to become a peateful democratic nation.
John K. Emmerson

Enolosure to Despatoh No. 51 of November 13, 1945 fr
the Acting United States Political Adviser to the Sap-

MEMCRANDUM OF CONVERSATION
November 5, 1945.
l'articipants: Kyuichi TOkUDA, leader of the Japanese
Communist Party
E. Herbert NORVN
John X. Emmerson.
Subject: Comnunist Party Policy and Current Japanese
roblems.
Mr. Tokude called at his own request to discuss
three general topics: 1) Communist Party policy and
present problems facing Japan; 2) recent strikes of
Korean coal miners; and 3) hidden Japenese Government
documents. Information provided on the latter subject
has been turned over to ap ropriate officers in the
Counter Intelligence Section. The following is a sum-
mary of .r. Tokuda's remarks on points on 1) and 2)
above.
Primery Economic Problems: Food, Unemployment, Land
The three immedi:te issues in which the Communist
!erty is most concerned are food, unemployment, and land.
Japenese capitelists are now sebotaging production,
hoarding materials, and purposely encouraging inflation.
Many workers were dismissed at the end of the war with-
out compensation. Employers hold rations destined for
the workers in sddition to their profits made during the
wer. Relexation of controls has boosted already rising
prices which mean edded profits for the capitelists.
Laborers' weg-s have been reduced to t their war-
time level. Unskilled workers now reoeiving ¥2 per day
and skilled workers receiving ¥5 were paid four times
those amounts before the end of the war. On the other
hand, food prices have skyrocketed. Rice in Tokyo is
selling for ¥70 per sho (3.18 pints); in Osaka and Kyoto
the price is ¥l20 per sho. The explanation for extra-
ordinerily high prices in ryoto is the fact that the city
was undamaged and has attracted large numbers of wealthy
citizens and nembers of the nobility.
orkers who received discherge ellowances upon dis-
missal have lergely spent this money by now. The unem-
ployed have drifted easily into black market operations.
Foodstuffs and other commodities now purchasable in ru-
ral districts for sale in the cities will be exhausted
by the end of the year. Unless some remedial measures
are taken, there is great danger that riots will break
out as conditions becone worse.
Members of the imperial family and the nobility still
hold large quantities of food and materials. While nation-
al forests

=== Page 286 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
al1
al forests were nearly cut during the war, onormous
tracts of untouched forest land are held by the Im-
perial Household. Outstanding among these are lands
in Kiso, Ameki,in Izu, Ishikarikawa and Niikappu in
Hokkaido. Utflization of these forests would provide
adequste timber for the reconstruction of private homes
destroyed durin the war.
At the end of the war, he Japanese Army possessed
three years' stocks of food (rice), clothing, medicine,
tobacco, and other materials. Junior end non-oommis-
sioned ofric-rs seized huge quantities of these goods
and secreted them. Supplies thus far recovered by
United States Arny units amount to a small fraction of
the totel amount. The followin are specific examples:
Capt. Kunimesa, Director of Supply Department, lst
Naval Medical Arsenal, meguro, Tokyo, in company with
20 officers, seized l&rge supplies of drugs and set up
a pharmeceutical manufacturing company (Toyo Shinyaku
abushiki eisha). Sup lies are said to have been se-
creted in the warehouses of the Workers' Training Ins-
titute (Rodo Kunren-sho), within the Ministry of Wol-
fere. Other supplies were stored at the Tsurumi Dock-
yards in Kawasaki, near Yokohama. The numerous ins-
tances of hoarding sup lies indicete a deliberate po-
licy of the militerists at the present time.
There are actutlly sufficient food stocks in the
country et present. Landlords are selling only part
of their rice to the Governaent and are hoarding the
remainder. Farners are aware of this and are inoreas-
ingly reluctent to sell their own supplies whioh they
feel will be hir e y the privileged classes and not
equitably distributed to the general population.
Comnunist cono nic I roposals
1
The Communists ake the following proposels to
solve the food problem: 1) Place under imnediate con-
trol for the people's use hoarded foodstuffs in the
possession of the Imperial household, militarists cap-
italists, and landlords; 2) Institute a rigorous ins-
pection and tnorough survey of all stocks of foodstuffs
in the country. Such a survey should be carried out
by committees orgenized by the farmers; 3) In the cities,
institute inspection of factory werenouses by factory
comnittees and representstives of labor unions; 4, Re-
place the present neighborhood associations (tonari-gumi

The Popular Front
The Comaunists envisege three stages in the esteb-
lishment of e populer front with the Socialists.
1) Cooperation witi. the lower strate of the Socia-
lists towerd soluticn of the problems affecting the
people's livelihood. Such a commong front has already

tion between Socielists end Communists hes been estab-

recent strike undert&ken at the Tsurumi Dockyards was
successful and wes led jointly by Socialists and Com-
munists. The leaders of the Social Democretic Party
oppose such a policy but the lower strats of members
support it.
2) The Communists clan to make 8 united front with
the Socielists toward the sol tion of specifio prob-
lems: food, unemplovment, lend reform, wages, war cri-
minals.
I rospects are especi&lly hopeful for unity on the
question of the punishment of wer criminals. Socialists
and Communists will participate in a mass meeting to be
held in anda on November 7 to commemorate liberal lea-
ders who heve been victims of political oppression. On
December 8, anniversary of Pearl Harbor, it is planned
to hold a joint meeting at wnich time the united front
on the question of war crininals is expected to be com-
plete.
3) Abolition of the mperor system and establish-
nent of a Po∞ple's Republic.

Korean Coal Miners' Strike
There hes been some unfortunate misunderstanding
on the part of Americen authorities with regard to the
recent strike of Korean coel miners in Fukushima Pre-
feoture. At the end oi the war, hinese and Korean
laborers, who hed been long oppressed by Japanese autho-
rities, reacted egainst the Jepanese and seized stocks
of food and other meterials. This was regarded as a
rict by mericen Army authorities.
The Communist !'arty policy is opposed to anerchis-
tic uprisings. It fevors protests and strikes carried
out by peaceful, legal means.
When e meeting of oreens and Japarese wes held in
the villege of Taire, it was evidently interpreted as
an instigetion to riot by Americen Army officers. It
wus, however, a politicel meeting. Interpreters were
selected by capitalists who wented to suppress the
strike and misunderstending wes ceused by inacurrate
transletions.
The Comaunists fuel keenly the necessity to in-
creese coal production. lowever, the living conditions
of the miners hi ve been such thet they must make efforts
to im rove them. Their only recourse is to strike in
order to better their Iivin standards and consequently
to increase production.
Mine owners have consistently broxen the criminal
code by beating laborers, forcing them to work, and in
sone ceses killing those who resisted. On numerous
occasions gendarmes have driven the workers at the point
of beyonets. At present miners must buy their food at
black merkct prices, since they are receiving only half
the rations alloted to them.
To improve treir conditions, laborers must be given
freedom tounionize themselves and to elect shop commit-
tees to psrticipate in the distribution of food and clo-
thing.
The Communists

=== Page 290 === (confidence 0.98)
6
The Communists have instructed workers not to re-

tances. Strikes have occurred in Hokkaido, Ashio (cop-
per mines), Yameguchi, and Kyushu (coal mines). Com-
munist representetives have proceeded to these places
to attempt to work out an understanding.
There now exist three orean groups in Japan. The
first is the Korean Federation (Chosen-jin Renmei). A
reactionary group of Koreens, most of whom were for-
merly employed by the Japanese Government, were expelled
from the Federation and have now formed an organizetion
called the Regeneration Society (Kosei Kai). They ao-
cuse the Federetion of being Communistic which 1s un-
true. While there are Communists in its membership,
it is not a Communist orfanization. A third group of
Koreans, described as "bourgeois", is the Rolier So-
ciety (Kyuen Kai). The Communists esk that the Fedo-
ration be rearded as the representative Korean or-
genization in Japan.
The Communists propose thet orean soldiers and
forced laborers should be repetristed to Korea immo-
di&tely. However, long-time oreen residents in Japan
snould be siven opportunity to close their business
affairs and to sell their property before being returned
to Koree. Furthermore, Koreans should be permitted to
take more than ¥1,000 out of the country which is the
present restriction set by Jepanese Government regula-
tion. Many Koreens are forced to sell their property
at a loss because of tnis limitation to ¥1,000.

TRANSLATION
THE RULING CLASSES OF JAPAN
by M. Sokolov
Iublished in Red Fleet, Auguet 30, 1945
As is kmown, the olitical nstitutions of contemporary
Je an arose on the basis of the Constitution of 1889. This
Constitution :s not the fruit of original creation of Japanese
legielntors. In the l7o's a special mission was sent to Europe
with the task of aseimilating everything in other countries which
 ght bo useful for the state stmucture of Japan.
As n recult of tl le contact, not a progreseive constitution
but t obsolete Frueg'an Constitution, which granted practically
unlim'ted rigl ts to the Fperor, was adopted as the model for the
bac'c ntate law.
In general, the Japenese most read!ly adopted that which they
caw in Gormeny. Thus, for oxample, the Gorman system was the
besis for the organizat on of the Japanese army in the 188o's.
All thie cannot be considered tu. accident of history. The
cist:nctivo feature: of tre Fruosian steto system fully met the
interests of the bloc of great landowners and capitalists which
ruled and rulee conte orary Jalan. The cepitalist development,
startin; in the pecond half of the last century, proceeded rapidly.
The wers againet China and Tsarist Russia gave a strong stimulus
to the development of Ja anose industry. At the same time, the
proceee of concentrat on of industry and banking capital was
accelerated.
But even so, the country was not freed from survivals of
feudalium. In Japan we cee a fantaetic interveaving of imperialism
with rermants of feudal relationships. This interweaving ie seen
both in the economic and the olitical life of the country. The
landless and virtually landle: c peasantry--three-fourths of the
rural : o ulat'on--remains n slavieh dependency upon the landlords,
kuleke, and usurors. The ecalee and forms of tlls slavery are mon-
strous. The indebtedness of tle peasant farmers, except for the
kulake, grows fron year to year. Famine-etricken regione of
hundreds of thoueands and even millions of inhabitants are facts
of Japaneve reality which have long since ceased to cause surprise
e'ther wit.in the country or abroad.
Ti.e wages of Japanese workers, even higlhly qualified ones,
have always been extremely low.
All this taken together Ias made the internal market of Japan
very limited in purchasing power. The half-starving Japanese
peasant has nevor worn any foctwear other than sandals on wooden
soles. The consumption of textiles, sugar, and other products of
tho light and food induetries Is inconsequential.
The

AIR MAIL
-2-
The growing disproportion betweon the level of development
of industry and tle restricted intermal market, which has under-
¿ono ractically no expans:on in the course of decades, inten-
sified the policy of conquost of the Japanese ruling cirgles.
All tie wmrs of Jaran--the eeizure of tho broadest holdings
in China, in other arts f Asia, on the Pacific--have been wars
for now selling markete, new rourcoe of raw ateriale.
All these fecte end henonone chould be borme in mind when

Whatever Jaycnose Jurists may vrite, tho whole state system of

and internal contradictions of the country; the practice of state

The procent Japanesc Const tution ay be considered even

Its tirc allured the Japanece lcgiclators. Actuelly, the funda-

The C mct:tt en emphac:zos the "divine origin" of the
Lunpcror. To ey ress doubt of thir--in the prosc, in the universities,
ir. scicntific work--means to comait  cri o ageinst the Constitution.
Gnly e few yeare ago rofessor Minobe, who dared to write in
one of his werke that "the Maperer is alco on organ of authority",

The "nobe "cace" von reviewed in various stages. one of the

Thir story  a cur'ous llustrat:on of the cystem of con-
tony orary Japancce "law and order".
Tie structure cnd activity f the Tarliamont also reflect to
no oall extent the interweaving of imperialism with the remnants
of feudal relationehi e which wo obscrve in Japanese life.
The Parlia ant, first convened in 190, ic divied into two
houces.
The ur nor houce ("Toers") ineluder ored:tary membere, those
ai ointed by tic Emperor, and elected members.
The first category includee princet of perial Llood, all
the princes and marguisos. Mombers of the cecond category are
appointed personally by the Emperor. The piglt of clection to
the u per houen is enjoyed only by tery large taxpayers fron threc
large cities end 4? prefectures.
A total of onc ercent. of the population of Japan voted in
the first electione to the lower house of the Japanese Parliament,
which tock place over a half century ago. But subsequently, in
opite of the much-publicized "broadening of voting rights", the
results of elections to the lower houec reflected the opinion of
only a very small part of the populet1on of Japan. In 1928, 12
or

=== Page 315 === (confidence 0.98)
AIR MAIL
or 1? percent of the nopulation took part in the elections. A
whole series of legal and property limitations practically made
:t impossible for broed etrate of the population to participate
in the elections. The impoaing money tax which has to be paid by
a candidate for election fives access to the right of election
only to reprosentatives of political parties which possess con-
eiderable reane. If one recalls in addition the corruption,
bribery, pressure from the administrative machinery, then the
conditions of tre elections, limited by the Conetitution, may lese
than ever be coneidered an act of "free expreeeion of will".
Apart fron the upper and lower rouses of Parliament, there
are two further inetitutions. The firet of these is the "Privy
Council" composed of renresentatives of classes which were the
most influential feudal lorda of Janan before the coup of 1868.
This Privy Council hes the right to internret the Constitution
and alao all new and old lawe.
There in no mention in the Conetitution of the second of
these organe--the so-called "Gonro" Inrtitute. It is a counoil
of elder etatesmen who at one time or another have headed the
government. In recent yeare only one member of this council--
the 90 year old rince Saionji--has survived. The others have
died out.
The opinion of the "Genro" has frequently had more weight
in the eyes of the Emperor than the decisions of the Cabinet
of Ministers.
From the beginning of the present century, we invariably
see representatives of two influential parties--Seiyukai and
Minseito--in the lower house. It is diffioult to draw a sharp
line between these two parties, between their political programs.
The difference perhaps consists in the fact that the Seiyukai
are more closely connected with the big landowners than the
Minseito. But much more important for understanding the sosial
nature of these two political parties is the fact that they are
both connected with two monstrously overgrown conoerns which
have swallowed up large and small enterprises in the most varied
fields of industry, banks, newspapers, entertainment enterprises,
etc. Actually, the Seiyukai is the political representative
of the Mitsui concern and the Minseito of the :iftsubishi concern.
Both these parties carry on their work to a large extent with
subsidies received from the Miteui and Mitsubishi concerns.
In the past, representatives of the Seiyuai and Minseito
have formed the Cabinets of Ministers, in proportion to the
division of seats in the lower house. But even at that time
the Japanese Parliament did not determine the political line
of the Government. Moreover, such activities of Parliament,
according to the conceptions of Japanese logislators, would
oven have been a "violation of the constitution!, for, according
to the Constitution, the Government beare no responeibility
before Parliament.
In the early thirties, after Japan started the seizure of
Manchuria, the Cabinete of Ministers were formed not from repre-
sentatives of parliamentary parties, but from "non-party" figures--
generale

In 1936 came still another attempt to copy the "Gorman model".
Like Hitler, the militarists attempted to seize all authority by

UJ Cl: Japanese Political Parties (Report for Week Ending November

Recommendations as to Registration of Parties, etc., and Pro-
hibitions against Certcin Candidates.
Hereunder cur seventh weekly report on Japanese political parties
submitted pursuent to the Chief of taff's memorandum of October 3, 1945.
In view of the approeching elections and the large number of Diet
members who are expected to run again and who are tainted by their past
activities, we also submit a number of recommendations which seem per-
tinent et this time.
e recommended in our memorendum of November 9, 1945, that present
Liet members who were fornerly mombers of associations listed in Part I,
Paragraph 5- of the ovevall Directive (SNNCc 52/7) be prohibited by in-
formal action from standing for re-election. Now that the elections are
so close, the new Progressive Party organized and the campaign underway,
it may not be advisable to undertake such a sweeping restriction, and we
accordingly modify our recommendation to include at this time only "in-
fluential members" of the organizations specified in the overell direc-
tive. In order to accomplish this purpose it would seem to be necessery
to obtain a roster of the parties which could be accomplished by requir-
ing t eir registraticn with the Government of pertinent details, includ-
ing sources of their funds. (It is wilely reported by Japenese that
larze sums for the Liberal and Progressive Parties are being secretly
furnished by wealthy reactionaries.) pecifically we recommend that:
1. Each political purty stould bo required to file with the
Japenese Government by an early specified date a declaration of
ite purpose, names of officers (including a statement of each
officer's previcus affiliations with parties and organizations),
roster of membership, and source of funde.
2. That its immediste dissolution should he ordered if Head-
tion, that any party is  "successor" to one of the organization
specified in the irective.
3. That the Japanese Government should be informed (orally)
that no person should be permitted to become a candidate for the
Diet in the coming election who hes been an influential member of
one of the organizations specified in the Directive. By "influen-
tial" should be meant officer, director, committee chairman or
important member.

the former Imperiel Rule ssistance Association, the Imperial Rule As-

I heve the honor to trensmit a copy of a memorandum of a joint
interview held at General Headquarters on November 17 with Mr. TSUmUMI
Yusuke, Diet member and one of the leaders of the new Japen Progressive
Party (Nippon Shimpo-to). Mr. Emmerson of this Mission participated in
the interview.
Mr. TSURUMI hes had extensive experience as a lecturer in the
United States where he defendedJapen's wer against China in 1937. He
speaks excellent English, has a disarming personality, and knows how
to present his case in the most favoreble light for Americans. He
reiterated during his interview that he understood the directness of
Americans and wented to keep Headquerters constently informed of the
developments within his party. He stated he was ready to answer any
nd all questions with complete frenkness.
Summary. The Progressive Party believes in economic freedom but
to the extent advoceted by the Liberals who cling to l9th century
as of laissez-faire. The Progressives went to substitute "pesple's
onomous control" for bureaucratic control. They support a con-
tutional menarchy similar to that of Great Britain. The party
lizes that its future depends on the issue of war responsibility.
this issue is not pressed too herd, the party hopes to obtain a
yorking majority of 233 in the next Diet. TSURUMI's formula was to
exclude from the party 1) all members of wer cabinets, and 2) TOJO's
henchmen. The second condition has been accepted but the first has
not been carried out, since it would exclude such persons as MACHIDA
Chuji, "grand old man" of Jepenese politics. Without an army end with
e disrupted police force, Japan now is ripe for politicel disturbance.
There is greet nood for a solid stabilizing politicel organization
whith the Progressive Party hopes to provide. End of Summary.
Respectfully yours,

After initiel contact with Yusuke TSURUMI, Member of the Diet

Adviser; Mejor Roest, Government Section; Lt. Damsgaard, Civil

two hours of interrogeti on.
ORGANIZAT ION OF THE SHIMPOTO
1. The SHIPOTC wes officiell orgenized on 16 November, at
e generel meeting of its adherents mong members of the Diet. Approx-
imetely 230 members of the lower house heve joined the party. Ail are
former members of the DAI NIPPON SEIJIKAI, which had 380 members in
the House.
2. A president, or netional leader, of the party has not yet
been chosen, though TSURUMI hes e cendidete in mind. Two top posts
et present are those of Chief whip, held by TSURUMI, end Reseerch
Director, held by OTA Tekao SAITo is usuelly cheirman of perty
meetings, is best-known of their public figures.
3. Sarlier, it hed been ennounced that the new perty would be
called "/INHONTO". An explanetion of the reason for the change is of
politicel interest. A debete developed inside the group as to which of
two elternative Japenese equivelents of the term "democrecy" wes to be
used. Some, including TSURUMI, wented to use "MINSHUTO" which implies
people-es-master in the state; others fevored "MINHONTO" which implies
people-as-basis of the state. Those supporting the latter threetened
a split if "MINSHUTO" were used. TsURUMI considered thet unwise and
proposed "SHIMPOTO" s e compromise which mede no commitments es to
the locus of sovereignty. (TSURUMI's modificetion of populer
sovereignty: Jappnese government should be by the people, and for
the people, but not of the people.)
4. It is unfortunate that the origins of SHIMPOTo were identified
in the press in terms of the MACHIDA-NAKAJIMA factions. MACHIDA is a
"grend old men" of the movement, ebout 83 yeers old. But NAKAJIMA is not
acceptable to the new perty, end will retire from politice.
5. Al though

=== Page 335 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
5. Although the MINSEITO end SEIYUKAI perties are formally non-
existent, they still heve deep roots locelly. The MINSEITO element
predominetes in the SHI.POTO, representing three-fifths of its perlia-
mentery membership.
PROGRAN
6. The platform of the new group wes written by TSURUmI and
Vaseteke OTA (& former Director of the IRAPS). Some of the policies
are considered redical, but none directly effect the monarchy system,
support of which is fundement:l to the SHIMPCTO. The basic tems of
religious end politicel freecom ere espoused, including the revisions of
the electoral lew (lowred ege, women suffrege) now supoorted by the
government.
7. It is on the question of economic freedom thet radical
divergences among the new parties occur. The SHIMPOTO recognizes that
economic liberclism of the l9th-century veriety no longer suffices.
Economic controls must be meintained, endin this respect the progrem of
the SHIMPOTO eporoaches the SHAKAITO rether than the JIYUTO. The
people, however, will heve to be sold on the idee thet controls in
economic life ere necessery. They bitterly dislike the old bureaucracy-
ridden system. It is necessary to put forwerd & progrem of control by
the people. This mey be done through such messures as consumer-perticipe-
tion in the retionin: edministrstion.
SHIMPOTC is for  controlled economy within the system of
privete ownership. This jistinguishes it from the JIYUTC, which wents
to eliminete controls, es well es from the SHAKAITO, which seeks the
extension of government ownership in industry end finence. SHIMPOTO
is neither for nor pgeinst nationalizstion in principle, but will decide
epch crse on its merits. A resesrch bureru, to investigate just such
problems, is being estsolished under the leadershio of OTA.
9. Agriculture snd lebor orogrems re es yet undeveloped. In
the letter field, the J'IMPOTo hes "few exnerts."
10. Constitutional reform:  aintrin a constitutional monerchy,
tending towerd the Pritish system; Pprlisment to have central place,
Cebinet to be responsible to Parliament; Privy Council system to be
revised in the direction of lecrersing its nower, limiting numbers end
tenure, but not ebolition; oureaucr cy to be overhsuled. The SHIMPOTO
hes teken no stand on revision of rticles of the constitution which
guarentee the sovereignty of the Tenno.
WAR-RESPCNSIBILITY
1l. It is recognized thet the problem of wer-responsibility is
perheps the primary issue fecing the new party. The party as e whole
cennot escepe the tsint of heving been involved in responsibility for
the wer. At the seme time, they mey be able to maintein a large pert
of their power if they find & wsy to exclude elements most responsible
for the wer, most cleerly identified in the public mind with prosecution
of the wer nd responsibility for the defeet. TSURUMI's formule wes to
exciude from the SHIMPOTO (1) s1l members of eny wer cabinet; (?) TOJO's
henchmen. In fect, however, the first condition wes abandoned, beceuse
they did not went to draw a line which would exclude men like MACHIDA,
who wes & member of the KOISo cebinet. But the second condition wes
accepted, resulting in the exclusion of the aircreft-manufecturer
Chikuhei NAKAJIMA Ps well as Tedao OASA.
12. It is hoped

12. It is hoped that the cheracter of the perty in relation to
war-responsibility will be more cleerly defined when a president is
selected. TSURUMI wants e man who will be strong enough to say that

of more Diet members. The compromise cheracter of the party at present

piven the honorery desinetion of "advisers".
13. The NCW JAPAN CONS RUCTION PARTY, 8 group of younger members

of these veople were linked to the war-responsible elements which TSURUMI

14. In generel, while there cre still undesireble elements in the

cool reception by the public is more or less ineviteble. The whole
question of wer-responsibility mey compromise the SHIlPoro seriously.
OTLR COMMENT
15. The next Diet will look something like this: 50 for the
SHAKAITC: 100-120 for the JIYUTO; 200-plus for the SHIMPOTO; rest
scrttered. ?33 seets ere requirei for e working majority. A coalition
of the SHIMPOTC with the JIYUTO is possible.
16. The sHIIPOTO is unpopular with the press right now. The ex-
pulsion of the old directors and editors hes been pertly responsible
for this. Some of the young journelists who heve instigeted the ex-
pulsions hed formerly hobnobbed with ermy officers. ASAHI's Bunjiro
SUZUKI is & liberel, & friend of TSURUMI's, whose support he hed
counted on. The reletive svece given to the SHIMPOTO and, sey, the
Communists. is unfeir. The SHIMPOTC hes also been discriminated ageinst
in the rrdio field. There is a generel leftwerd trend now in the redio
end press, end the SHIMPCTO suffers somewhet for it.
17. The present government is too weak to corry out eny reel
policies. HIDEHARA is too fer removed from the people.
18. In general, the SHIMPOTO has the tesk of functioning es 
stebilizing oolitical center in a country ripe for politicel disturbence.
It is a country long repressed, with no tredition or experience in
democretic self-discipline, from which the lid hes been suddenly re-
moved.

III. DEMOBILIZATION AND DISARMAMENT
Demobilization of the Japanese home forces was accomplish-
ed in the short scace of two months. of the 2,186, 000 men
under arms withi the Ja anese main islands on August 15 only

War Responsibility
Since the opening of the Diet session it has been clear that the
first issue of importance to come to s vote would be the question of
war responsibility. Onl, a handful of Diet members have resigmed and
the efforts of the party men seem to have been concentrated on find-
ing a formule which would satisfy public demand for clarification of
iet responsibility in the wer and st the same time not destroy their
chances in the next clection.
It was at first hopel that the part ies could agree on one resolu-
tion but it soon tecame clear that this was impossible. The question
was scheduled for debate in the House of Representatives on November
3o tut no conclusior was reached in conmittee and the metter was post-
poned until Decenber 1 when two resolutions were introduced.
The firet rosclu'ion was sigued by twenty members of the Liberal
Party end supported by the Sociel bemoorats. It stated thet the House
ofRepresentatives could not cerry on its work without first elerify-
ing the question of war responsihility ana that those leaders in the
wertime Diet who wholeheartedly supported the Government should feel
their responsibility and decide their fut ure course of ection. The
second resolution introduced ty the Progressive Party divided war res-
ponsibility into two cetegories, those who initisted en ill-advised
wer and those who violsted nternationel lew during the wer. It ab-
solve the generel publie who followed ordere of the Government and
ended rather weekly, "e legisletors should also reflect upon our
pest conduct calmly nd exercise self-repentance and proceed with the
work of reconstructiy the country."
When vote was finally aken et 4:15 p.m. on December 1, the Pro-
gressive resolution w pesse? by  vote of one hundred seventy-eight
votes ageinst forty-five votes for the Liberel resolution. At least
one hundred of the Progres ivet as well as s good many Liberals end
Sociel Democrets absteineu fron voting.
Phe Indep ent Club, numbering only ton or twelve members, op-
posed both resolution end its members left the House chamber bofore
the vote was taken. They heve prepored a dreft resolution and will
propose resienution of all pembers of the present Diet following pas-
sage of the olection refor. bill. They state that the present Diet
is not qualified to teke up such messures es the land reform bill and
the labor union bill. Their political purpose is clearly to encourage
the splitting of the Progrescive Party which appeers likely at the
present time.
General reaction to the Diet di cussion of wer responsibility is
that Hous• members are obviously reluctent to vote themselves out of
office and that they will herdly of their n accord come to a doci-
sion which will guarentee a thoroughly renovated Diet efter the next
election. A SCAP directive would clesr the atmosphere by setting que-
lifications for candidates for the next House of Representatives. It
is believed thet such s directive would be welcomed by many of the
Diet members themselves and by the Japanese public in goneral.

FOREIGN ACTIVITY CORRELATION
Department of Stat
December 6, 1945.
MEMORANDUM
TO: John Carter Vincent, Director
Office of Far Eastern Affairs
101945
FROM: R. E. Carroll
SUBJECT: Report on Japan
I forward the enclosed report with the idea that
it may be of some assistance to you in your work.
894.00/12-645
Many reports have probably been written about this
visit and some of the facts set down therein will
probably be well-known to you. However, I submit the
report for what it may be worth to you.
This report may be slightly different in that
it is not an interrogation destined to bring out
intelligence, but it is a purely voluntary report
which these men rendered to the Y.M.C.A. in New York,
which has a great many interests in Japan and Korea.
They obtained the services of these men to speak
at this conference in order to get first-hand reports
on purely Y.M.C.A. matters. The report, therefore,
is voluntary and of a religious nature, but at the
same time valuable in furnishing background informa-
tion as to conditions in Japan and Korea and the
attitudes of the people there.
C

=== Page 378 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
REPORT ON JAPAN
Made by four religious leaders who just returned from Japan
November 27, 1945, 156 Fifth Avenue, New York City.
(Notes taken by Frank B. Lenz, International Seoretary of Y.MC
Dr. Douglas Horton
We were carrying out the purpose of the Riverside
Conference in California 1n 194l. In September 1945
we heard a broadcast from Radio Tokyo saying a visit
would be welcome. We flew from San Francisco and spent
23 days in Japan.
We were not representatives of Mission Boards,
nor denominational groups. Nor did we go as Americans
but as Christians. Four men went. We hoped for a
Canadian and some women.
Dr. Schaefer and I went to Korea but we had not
planned that originally. Even General Hodge said it
would be misunderstood if we did not go.
Korea is divided by the 38th parallel. It is a
political as well as a geographical line. A heavy
curtain has been dropped by the Russians. Koreans. are
slipping from North Korea to the south. Minerals are
in the north; agricultural lands and business in the
south. The Koreans are dismayed about this division.
In the south, although there are 70 parties there
are only 2 parties. Our military men thought that
leadership could be built around Rhee and Kim. We
expect better conditions in a few months.
Things are run down in Korea. The water mains are
clogged. Severance Hospital is dirty and run down.
One said it was a pig pen. An army man said it was
worse a month ago. A new Board has been installed.
No running water, no sheets and no anesthetics. Fami-
lies move in to cook. It is a mess. Doctors and
nurses are needed now.
Helen Kim is still at Ewa and is still the same
Helen Kim. She stood off the Jap military as long as
possible. The college is in fair condition. Registra-
tion is up to pre-war. Chosen Christian College is
also in fair shape,
but.the windows are broken. New
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 379 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
-2-
leadership is needed.
In Japan the chaplains, in their relation with
the Japanese Christians, are splendid. In the
devastated cities little contact was made with the
Christians. They are scattered. A young G.I. saw
a church steeple and went in. He met there a group
of Christian ministers in session. He was moved to
stand and say: "Nobody sent me but I want to bring
greetings from American Protestants". An older
replied and received him gladly. A chaplain (Pott)
in Doshishe University was very popular.
A group of W.C.T.U. people went to the airfield
and talked with the American flyers on politics and
religion.
Midu Kawai is still there. Her record still
holds. She refused to let her girls march in a
victory parade. She raised a fund to start an agri-
cultural college for women. She said they found
Christian Americans through the G.I.'s.
Miss Fugits is a great woman suffragist. The
new laws will enable women to vote and hold office.
The suffragists want Miss Kawai to stand for Diet.
A league of women voters has been formed. There is
a new day for women in Japan.
The president of the Y.W.C.A. is active and
will be more aggressive.
Most of the kindergartens were burned as were
several of the training schools. Christians started
again the kindergarten work.
The Y.W.C.A. magazine was stopped because it
was international.
The Y.W.C.A. lost many buildings but the big
building in Tokyo is in use. Our Navy took it with
the exception of 4 rooms. Their rest cottages are
in use.
The women's groups started a move to sell
valuable personal things in return for food. We
brought back kimonos and obi's as a token of the
women's friendship.
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 380 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
-3-
Dr. Van Kirk
I had never been to Japan. I was made business
manager and had to do a lot of odd things. Dr. Gulick
had engendered in me a love of Japanese. We were
billeted in the Disahi Hotel in Tokyo.
Dr. Schaefer and I got up at daybreak and walked
around the city for 2 hours. Ruins everywhere, but
saw a few little gardens - symbolical. Talked with
an old elder in the Genza church; at 5:30 met Rev. Metsui
and his family and learned of the plans to rehabilitate
the church.
Saw American G.I. guarding the Imperial household,
a lesson in humiliation.
We didn't know how the Japanese Christians would
receive us. We met a band of Christians in the Y.M.C.A.
informally, therefore genuine. Mr. Saito brought some
leaders of the Church of Christ. There were many tears
shed at that little meeting. This little meeting
resolved differences. Met Kagawa in the evening. He
prayed: "Now that the war is over let the sun shine
down upon they work again, etc." Then we had a commu-
nion service.
Russell Durgin of the Y.M.C.A., now of the State
Department, was of great help: He prepared most of
our meetings. We got army bread for the communion.
The Christians in Japan didn't know what the
churches in the U.S. had been doing. We told them of
Japanese evacuation from the Pacific Coast. Told them
of Dulles work with the Church on Just and Durable
Peace. Many wept at these reports and said: "This is
happiest day of my life." "We see again with our own
eyes our friends from U.S."
Kagawa has lost one fourth of his weight. Clothes
don't fit, somewhat ragged but filled with enthusiasm
for Christianity.
In the second year of war, he was imprisoned a
day in Kobe. A half year later he was questioned day
and night for 9 days. A student told the police.
Later he was released and allowed to preach in his
own church but could not move around. He said:
"MacArthur is a good man. The G.I.'s won us. If we
had been victorious At, would have,been a catastrophy
CONFIDENIIAL

=== Page 381 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
-4-
for us and the world. Our culture had fallen to a low
ebb."
He is again carrying on great evangelistic meetings.
One meeting registered 150 people signed to become
Christians. He went with us to visit Hogach1 Kuni.
Kagawa said the Emperor's brother was making a study of
Christianity. He wanted to read Kagawa's new books.
He is addressing l0oo teachers to hear him on moral
culture. He has never before talked to teachers, the
purveyors of Shintoism.
"We need prayers, Bibles and good missionaries"
from America, said Kagawa. He is re-vamping the Coopera-
tive Movement. Says there are 13,000 in existenoe.
He is on the Executive Committee of the Social Demo-
cratic Political Party. He brings into this party labor
and agricultural groups. Kagawa will not run for office.
He promised God he would preach the gospel. He says it
is imperative to keep the Emperor because there is
political chaos in Japan. The emperor is a unifier.
Kagawa is a mover in the International Peace Asso-
ciation Morality Revival Society, a Rural Watchmakers
Institute for discharged soldiers. He says the F.O.R.
is fine for Christians but I must go to the Shintoists
and Buddhists through the International Peace Associa-
tion. He is active in the Relief program. He is getting
the use of barracks for the homeless.
He sent his greetings to all friends in America.
Bishop Baker
The devastation is unbelievable. I saw the ruin
of Hiroshuma and Nagasaki as well as ruins of other
cities.
A letter from a leader said: "We are completely
smashed. We have no army, no navy, no houses, nothing."
MacArthur is a gift of God in my judgment. We
have a single occupation and a man at the head who
knows the Orient, who is humane and who has imagination.
We were his guests at the old American Embassy.
MacArthur opened his heart and mind for 3 hours but I
can't reveal it here.
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 382 === (confidence 0.99)
CÔNFIDENTIAL
-5-
MacArthur is not only a great general but he is a
statesman. Fundamentally he is a Christian. He is not
soft to the military and financial forces of Japan.
"We are here to sow an idea of freedom and democracy", said
MacArthur.
The American G.I.'s go all over Japan unarmed.
MacArthur has embarked on a great venture of trust. The
people who were filled with fear now go about with confi-
dence. MacArthur said he is proud of the American
soldier. "We cannot control them but they have an inner
control developed in the American home, school and
church." The soldiers are not plastered saints. Their
attitude toward the Japs is one of friendliness. Miss
Kawai said: "Your boys are funny but they are friendly
and wonderful. We must bring up our children like these
boys." The G.I.'s make a line to let the women get into
the trains.
The Japanese people react to American boys. "Our best
ambassadors are our American troops", said MacArthur.
MacArthur is surrounded by hundreds of American
civilians in uniforms. To those men is committed the
task of setting up a whole new school system but MacArthur
feels that the reforms must come up out of Japanese life.
I think we ought to have a long occupation to
restrain the vicious element in Japanese society. People
in the past have been shadowed by secret police. We need
enough soldiers to police, then must have advisors.
The audience with the Emperor did not come from the
American headquarters. We were received as individuals,
not as a deputation. The venocular press had many stories
about our presence in Japan.
I walked in, bowed, the Emperor held out his hand,
showed me to a chair and we sat down and talked. I
walked out - didn't back out.
The Emperor talked with us about Christianity.
Thanked us for schools and wanted us back. The door is
open in Japan for Christianity.
One American officer sent word to his wife to send
$1500 for missionary work in Japan.

=== Page 383 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
-6-
They want us back. The American government will
cooperate.
The Christians want again to enter into the world
wide Christian fellowship. We were received with open
arms. There is a new day.
Dr. Schaefer
The Christian Movement, how did it fare during the war?
We are convinced of the complete integrity of the
Christian church in Japan. There was persecution of
ministers. One minister said: "War is a sun. We are
not told what we ought to know." He was arrested, tried;
appealed his case to Supreme Court and acquitted in
April 1945.
The Christians were like the bamboo in a storm.
It bends but it doesn't break. The Christians held to
their essential loyalty. Many services were stopped but
people met in homes. Attendance dropped off because of
hard work but the churches held their meetings. Sunday
Schools were small.
We found no ministers who were defeated. They were
all underweight and tired. But they are a convinced group.
The church had centralized control exercised over it
by government. But a union had already been a fact if
the pattern was formal. This union was bomb-proof. The
United Church survived pressure from authorities. Local
churches were urged to continue meetings and not to get
lax.
The schools were under pressure by the government,
by parents, by people who urged that Bible reading and
chapel be discontinued. "Get rid of Christianity", in
our schools was the government propaganda. In some cases
the schools had to change their constitution but the
teachers held other small meetings.
One principal was called to the government. He said:
"I may not return but if you don't see me again you will
know I have been honest."
The Christian schools are in an excellent situation
to carry on.
There are only 9 churches in Tokyo intact out of 157.
Many pastors and teachera,are homeless.
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 384 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
-7-
Everywhere we went the Japanese say this is a
marvelous opportunity for Christianity. This is a
period for rebuilding a new Japan. "Japan must rise
up out of its despair with Jesus Christ", said one
pastor. It is the greatest time for evangelism.
Everybody is eager for English and Christianity.
There is also a great opportunity for missionary
work. Teachers are wanted back.
The United Church will not continue as it is.
But it will not break up into previous denominations.
There will be a reorganization with democratically
elected leaders. Or there may be a branch organization
set up with an inner unity holding them together. Or
there may be a Federation of Churches.
Among the over-age ministers there has been a
revolt against the hierarchy in Tokyo as in New York
or Boston.
One large movement as a democratically elected
assembly will emerge. But we will not return to
denominationalism.
There is discussion for a great Christian University,
also an urge for a Japanese American university.
The Christian movement needs 100,000 hymn books,
one million Bibles. Bibles have been burned during
the war. A great need for Christian books, newer
books, commentories and pamphlets.
Relief is needed now. The people say millions
will starve. They donTt need money but food and clothing.
No pastor wanted anything that the people don't get.
No priorities for Christmas. General starvation need
not exist. The situation is not desperate.
The church cannot undertake a program of mass
relief. The occupying power must do this. But there
is need for: a Church Relief program in Japan at once.
The people are on the edge of subsistence.
The atomic bomb to the Japanese people was just
another bomb. When 100,000 people died in the fire
from bombing in Tokyo in March it made a greater impres-
sion than the atomic bomb. There is resentment but
people are saying "If it were not for our military the
Americans wouldn't have come over

Enclomure No. 2 to despatch No. 98 dated December 13, 1945,
George Atcheson, Jr., Acting United States Political Adviser te
the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, Tokyo, Japan, e
subject "Transmitting Memoranda of Conversations between Primee

RESTRICTED
LEMORANDUM OF CONVERSATION
November 7, 1945
Participants:
Prince KONOYE;
Mr. USHIBA, who acted as
interpreter;
Mr. Bishop.
Subject: Some political developments in Japan in 1941.
The following is a resume of remerks made by Prince KONOYE
to Mr. Bishop in a personal interview on the above date.
Prince KONOYE discussed the events preceding his
resignation as Prime Minister in the Fall of 194l, •s-
pecially the proceedings of the Imperial Conference on
September 6, and subsequent developments in Japanese High
Policy. He explained that at the conference the decision
was reached that the conversations with the United States
should be continued, but that if by the first part of
October, 1941, orogress had not been made in the American-
Japanese conversations, end if it appeared impossible for the
Japanese to reach an a reement with the United States on
minimum Japanese demands, Japen should immediately reach
the decision to undertake preparetions for war against the
United States end the implementation of war plans.
Prince KONOYE continued that on October 12 the Japanese
Government held a confer nce to discuss whether in accordance
with the above decision taken at the Imperial Conference on
September 6, the Japanese Government should then decide that
it wes no longer reasonetle to expect that agreement with
the American Government could be reached on minimum Japanese
demands ana whether, if that decision were affirmative, the
Japanese should not toke the decision to prepare for war
with the United States and discontinue the conversations
which had been in progress until that moment. As Prime
Minister, KONOYE said that he firmly believed that an
agreement with the United States was still possible and
that the conversations should be continued in order to
achieve that agreement. It was alsohis belief that if
an agreement for the withdrawal of Japanese troops in China
could be made, the whole problem of American-Japanese re-
lations could be amicably settled. Prince KONOYE added
that the War Minister, Gen ral TOJO, was diametrically
opposed and argued that there was no longer any possibility
of arriving at an agreem-nt with the United States aid that
Japan should at once take the decision to prepare to go to
war with the United States.
Prince
