-2
Prince KOnOYE said that the fundemental disagreement be-
tween himself and the Wer Minister hinged on the problem of
withdrawel of troops from China. According to KOmoYE, Gemeral
TOJO contended that although he appreciated the Prime Mimister's
difficult tesk end his unguesticnable sincerity, it was not
possible for Japan to agree to the withdrawal of troope from
Chine for the reasons that it would utterly destroy the morale
of the Jepenese Army and people, that once Japan had yiolded
to American preseure, the United States would resort to more
and more strin ent and arrogant measures against Japan and in
the end nothing woul be left for Japan to do except to dofend
itself by arms which woulc be difficult with morale destroyed,
that even though : temporary settlen-nt of the "China Incidont"
were worked out, reletions between Japan and China would again
break down within two or three years, and that there were in-
ternal weeknesses in both the United States and Japan and
accordingly, it was the best time for Japan to make up its
mind to prepare for war against the United States. Prince
KONOY added that there were four subsequent convereations
between the Prime Winister and the War Minister, and that
failure to re: ch an agreement with the War Minister during
these talks forced Frime linister KONOYE to resign.
In explaining that Gen ral TOJO when he became Prime
Minister, in spite of the views which had just been revealed
continued the conversations with the United States and gave
the appearance of seeking a peaceful settlement of pending
problems, KONOY stated that in this connection the position
of the Navy was hihly important. He said that prior to his
resination as Prime Minister, the Japanese Navy had taken the
stand that it would agree to whatever course Prime Minister
KONOYE decided upon: If the decision were to continue the
conversations with the United States, the Navy would give whole
hearted sup ort; but if the Prime Minister were to decide to
undertake preperations for wer, the Nevy would not oppose that
course.
Prince KONOYE then gave the following description of
developments:
Through infcrmal but entirely reliable information,
the Prime Minister leerned that the Japenese Navy
was far from confident of Japanese ability to wage
a succeasful war agsinst the United States and that
the real desire of the most important leaders in
the Japenese Navy was to avoid war. The Army and
General TOJO were also informed of this basic
attitude on the part of the Navy. On October 13
or 14, 1941, the Gummu Kyoku Cho (Chief of the
Military Affairs Bureau) of the Army went to the
Chief Secretery of the Cabinet and told the latter
that the Army would not ive up its idea of pre-
paring for war immediately aginst the United States
if such were merely the wish of the Prime Minister.
However, the Army would consent to give up this pro-
gram if the Navy were formally to express its opinion
that the Japanese Navy was not prepared for war against
the United States. The Army therefore asked the Chief
Secretary of the Cabinet to obtain formally the offi-
cial views of the Navy Ministry. Consequently, the
Chief

=== Page 407 === (confidence 0.99)
94
-3
Chief Secretary called upon the Chief of the Military
Affairs Bureau of the Navy and aaked him to present
formally the official views of the Navy. The Naval
Chief of Military Affairs refused to consent to
this proposal and stated that the Navy. officially
end formally, would go no further than to agree to
leave the entire matter in the hands of the Prime
Minister and to support whatever decision the Prime
Minister should reach. The Army continued to oppose
the views of the Prime Minister and the Cabinet was
forced to resign.
With regard to the question of a successor.
it wae proposed et one time that Prince HIGAsHI-
KUNI should form a Cabinet in which the Army and
the Navy would be able to discuss the whole matter
with complete frankness nd come to an agreement.
The Emperor was reluctant to accept this proposal
and thereupon Marquis .IDO recommended General TOJO
as Frime Minister. It was Marquis KIDO's confident
belief thet TOJo hoving been informed of the real
views of the Navy and thoroughly realizing that
Japan could not reasonably undertake a Pacific
war with the Navy fundamentally opposed, would be
forced to reconsicer his view of a possible war
with the United States end exert every effort
to continue the converstions with the United
Stetes to = successful conclusion. The Emperor
himself, when he commanded TOJO to form a Cabinet,
expressed his merious desire thet General TOJO
shoulå make a "clean slate of the whole matter".
reconsider the questions from a new angle, and
continue the conversations et ell costs. In the
light of these circunstances, Prince KONOYE wes
also confident, at that time, that ToJo was sin-
cere in continuing the conversstions with the
purpose of avoiding war. In view of TOJO's in-
terpretaticn which he expressed before the Privy
Council of the November 26 (1941) note as an
American ultimatum it then became apparent that
the TOJO cabinet had given up further hope of
successfully continuing the conversations.
Prince KONOYE said that he could not offhand think of pe
cific or direct occesions in the gradual widening divergence be
tween the United States and Japan; but it seemed to him to be
the result of an accumulation of bad feelings and misunderstanding
ever since the Manchurian Incident of 193l. He did feel, however,
that on several occasions both governments could have exerted
more effective efforts to come to an understanding.
Prince KONOYE said that, for example, he believed that his
proposal for a meeting with the President of the United States
in 194l was one occasion when an understending might have been
reached and the question of withdrawal of Japanese troops from
China could have been satisfactorily solved. He explained that
it was his intention to meet the President in person and then
by telegraph to ask the Emperor to take a decisive step and to
order

order the withdrawal of troops from China. Prince KOmoYE went on

made when Japan decided to surrender. Prince KONoYE concluded
that he believed confidently thet through the direct intervention
of the Emperor the problem could have been solved at that time
although he fully anticipated t at he would have been assassinated
upon his return to Yokohama.

=== Page 419 === (confidence 0.97)
Enclosure No. 1 to Despatch No. 182 dated January 8, 1946,
from the United States Political Adviser, Tokyo, Japan, on
the subject: Japanese Amnesty of October 17, 1945.
MEMORANDUM
January 8, 1946.
SUBJECT: Japanese Annesty of 17 October, 1945.
The annesty, ordered on the occasion of Kanname-Sal
(Harvest Festival), was the third effected under the amnesty
ordinance promulgated September 26, 1912, the earlier two
having been ordered at the time of the funerals of the
Emperors Meiji and Taisho in 1912 and 1927, respeotively.
Previous to the promulgation of the amnesty ordinance
general pardons had been decreed on the occasions of the
Emperor Meiji's attaining his majority in 1869, at the time
of the promulgation of the Constitution in 1889, and on the
occasion of the death of the Empress Dowager Eisho in 1897.
The Imperial Rescript ordering the amnesty last October
follows:
"Wie, in the face of unprecedented
emergency place Our confidence in the support
of and collaboraiion of the public in general
and are seriously anxious that the whole nation
do their best to overcome the crisis. We here-
by especially order the officers concerned to
effect an amnesty. Ye, the officials and
public in general, take to heart Our Wishes."
The terms of the amnesty, as drawn up by the Justice
Ministry and approved by the Emperor, are summarized below:
I. PERSONS AFFECTED
Almost one million violators of the
following laws:
1. Criminal Law (including lese majeste,
insurrection, sedition, disturbance of the
peace, etc.)
2. Election Law
3. Peace Preservation Law
4. National Defense Law (including in-
subordination, aiding the enemy, eto.)
5. Law for the Control of Thought, Speech
and Press
BECEIAED
6. Army and Navy Criminal Law (including
release of secret information,
abel es NAL
PEB204
entrance of forbidden areas, etc.)
A89
7. Conscription

=== Page 420 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2-
7. Conscription Law
II. FORMS OF AMNESTY GRANTED
A. Complete Pardon (320,000 persons, of
whom 2,000 were in prison;
the rest were under various
forms of restriction)
Those in this category were freed, if they
were in prison, had the ir civil rights
restored and their criminal records des-
troyed.
B. Commutation (37,000 persons)
a) Death penalties changed to life imprison-
ment
b) Life imprisonments changed to 20-year
terms (life sentences of persons over
70 or under 16 on Sept. 2, 1945 shortened
to 15 years)
c) Term imprisonments changed as follows:
1. Term reduced by ? for those whose
sentences had not commenced
2. For those whose sentences had
commenced, the remaining period
was reduced by t, except that
those who had served less than
half of their terms had their
time reduced by only t
3. Terms of those over 70 or under
16 on Sept. 2, 1945 reduced by 1/3
No commutations were permitted for those
charged with:
1. High treason
2. Sedition
3. Incendiarism
4. Counterfeiting
5. Bodily injury to superiors
6. Aiding the enemy
7. Crimes against the laws of Korea,
Formosa, Kwantung and the South
Seas Islands
C. Restoration of Civil Rights (600,000 persons)
a) Those who had lost their civil rights or
had them suspended for crimes for which
BECEIAE
they completed prison sentences at least
5 years prior to Sept. 2, 1945
ofel es MAL
ER204
b) Those who had lost their civil rights or
1089
had them suspended for crimes for which
they

=== Page 421 === (confidence 0.96)
- 3-
they completed punishment not
involving prison sentences be-
tween Feb. 18, 1942 and Sept. 2,
l954, or who were conscripted for
military service
c) Those who had lost their civil
rights or who had them suspended
whose crimes were committed when
they were 18 or less and who com-
pleted their sentences or were
exempted before Sept. 2, 1945,
unless their sentences called for
death or life imprisonment.
Lists of those affected by the amnesty have not
been published and it is doubtful, in view of the large
numbers involved, whether such lists would be of value
for the interpretation of future political activity. To
the best of our knowledge, few if any political prisoners
liberated in accordance with the SCAP directive of October
4, l945, recently ordered reinstated as citizens in full
right, are taking an active part in politics except the
Communists, the more important of whose names are doubt-
less already familiar to the Department.

Preface
The Amegican-Japanese Negotiations during the Konoye Cabinet
were conducted over a long peried of from April, l941 to October
the same year. The talks at first were pushed under utter
secrecy, but as the talks leaked out, various cenjectures were
indulged in, and en the basis ef muoh conjectures all sorts of
attacks and criticisms were eepeentrated en the Gevernment.
I did net give up hepe uy te the last moment and seneentrated
my efforts en it. It wes beeause there were reasens fer which
an American-Japanese eenflict had te be aveided. First, the
outbreak of the Reioh-Soviet War. Secerd, the views of the Naval
leaders. Third, the matter of mmterials.
The Tripartite Alliance was oaneluded on the premise of the
German-Soviet-Japanese linking relation. But whon the Reich-
Soviet War breke out, thid premise was overturned. The Soviet
Union ran te the Anglo-American camps and our co ntry was placed
in the worst situation, a situation where this country might
be compelled to regard the United States and the Boviet Unien
as eur enemies. About this matter, I should like to refer you
to the sevarate article on " "Tripartite Alliance.
Admiral Oikawa
Approves Alliarce
The second iseue which conoerns the views of the Mavy. As
regards the conclusion of the Tripartite Treaty. at first I
thought that the Navy would not easily apprve the pact. This
nttitude of the Navy could easily be supposed judging from the
Navy's attitude thereon since the days of : he Hiranuma Cabinet.
Navy Minister Zengo Yoshida at the time of the Cabinet
organization agreed to the idea of etrengthening the. tripartite
Axis, but when the talk developed and the Tripartite 4lliance
with stipulations un military aid was proposed, it became a
big issue for the Navy. Navy Minister Yoshida seems to bave
become much worried. His heart trouble became worse and he was
forced te resign.
When Admiral Oikawa became the Navy Minister, the M oce
approved the Tripartite Alliance. I became suspicious the
abrupt manner in wich the Navy anproved the aliiance and inviting
the then Navy Vice-Mirister Toysda, inquired into the ciroumstances,
Vice-Minister Toyoda said:

Tripartite Pact, but since th domestic political conditiensno
longer permit further opposition on the part of theNavy, the
Navy unavoidably approves it, The Navy approves it for a
politieal reason. From the military standpoint, the Navy has
no such confidence as tofight the United States."
I said:
"This is something which is unexpecte to me. Politics is
something which we statesmen deal vith and the Navy reed not
have a concern in it. The Navy should examine the matter purely
from the military standpoint, and if it has no confidence,it•
should oppose it to the last. Isn't it the way to be loyal to
the country?"
Vice-Minister Toyeda said:
"Now that the situation has come to such a pass..
please have understarding for the position of the Navy. There is
no longer any other way except that of preventing the rise of the
obligation for military assistar ce in the Tripartite Pact by means
of dip) matic negotiations."
Shortly afterward, Admiral Iseroku Yamamoto, Commander-in-Chief
of the Combined Leet, came up to Tokyo and I saw him. The Admiral
was strongly opposed to the'Tripartite Alliance. The obstinate
opposition of the then Navy Minister Yonai to the Tripartite Alli n
in Hiranuma Cabinet days meemed to have be a strongly due to the
assistance of the then Vice-Min ter of the Navy Yamar.ote. When
1 moliicned to the Admiral that Vice-Mirister Toyola told me such
nd such things, the Admirl said:
"If I am told to fight regardless of consequence, I sha 
run wild considerably for the first six minths or a year, hut I
ave utterly r confidenc for the secend and third year fhe
Crpartite Pacthas peen conclujed and we cannet help it. Now
that the situaticn has come to this pass,pe ycu will endeavo:
"er aveilalo er n mericar-Ja ese war."
Thus, the i a of t' Nav ould oe scer i d. if the
mind of the Navy was such 1 co side ed the nct al appl:'is
of the Tripartite Pact  hac tr bo done with considerabl: cere
ar circumsroction.ven if th Sovie on took si'sw

=== Page 426 === (confidence 0.98)
Naval Leaders Still Cautious
When the American-Japanese Negotiations were opened, both
the Army and Navy enthusiastically hoped for the successful
conclusion of the talks. But when the morth of August came
the Army's enthusiamm began cooling. The views of the naval
leaders, however,. remained unchanged. I heard that naval
officers in the lower rankm began te raise strong views, but when
I msked the navsl leaders about it, they always replied

Liaison Conference
Immediately Held
In view of the importance of the issue, I called a liaison
conference between the Government and the Supreme Command at
8 o'cloek. The Government was represented by the Prime
Minister.. Home Minister, War Minister, Navy Minister, Vice-
Minister of Foreign Affairs, and the Supreme Command was

were also present. When the American preposal was made the
subject of talk, the following views prevaileds
1. Acceptance of this Ameriean plan would be the greatest
short-cut to the disposal of the China Affair. The establishment
of the Wang Gevernment failed to achi-ve anything and direet
negotiations with Chungking has become exceedingly difficult.
Since Chungking is now entirely dependent on the United States.
no direct negotiations with Chungking would accomplish anything
unless the United States is placed in the position of incer-
mediary. In view of this fact, the foregoing point is clear.
2. To respond to this proposal and seek rapprochement of
Japan and the United States would offer an excellent opportunity
for avoidance of an American-Japanese war. Moreover, it will
prevent the extension of the European War to the dimension of
a world war and may become the prelude to the bringing about
of world peace.
3. The national power of Japan today has been considerably
consumed. The China Affair, therefore, must be solved as soon
as possible, and efforts must be made to'restore and rear
the national powey. The idea of southward advance which is being
insisted on in some sections is impracticable. Even the Supreme
@ommand says it has no confidence nor preparations for it.
Even from the standpoint of rearing the national power, it is
necessary for this country to shake hands with the United States
at this time and seek to prcmote the replenishment of materials
for the future.

Matsueka Preposes

Irrespective of his outward speech and action, it was

Reioh-Soviet War Opens

Emperor Keserved Toward

the ships and the United States found it out and came to have
suspicion about the sincerity of our diplomacy. The lack
of unity between diplomacy and military affairs was the
source of trouble to me.

OCT 1 4 1948
Memorandum
DIVISION OF
5 February 1946
To: Mr. J. C. Vincent
From: O. Lattimore
Subject: Recent Ubservations in Japan and China.
The following notes are based on impressions formed
while I was with Ambussador Pauley's Reparations
Mission, and I have jotted them down for your possible
interest because they have nothing to do with the
problen of reparations, but may have some bearing on
overall trends in Asia.
3
1. Second Phase in Japan.
The first phase of occupation in Japan has been
ably carried out by Geneal MacArthur and his staff.
The Japunese are disarmed. There is no military danger
latent in Japan. This first phase may be concisely
described as the process of making the Japanese pow-
less to d) what we don't want them to do.
A second phase now opens, presenting the problem
of how to make the Japanese do what we do want them to
do. In this phase, the Japanese have considerable
ressurces of resistance, evasion, and even political
and psychological counterattack.
The essential fabric of the old structure of power
and rule in Japan remains intact. Those who enjoyed
power and rule will maneuver tenaciously to avoid
letting their privileges pass into other nands. It
is true that they are now the ruling class of a defeated
a.d mucn weaker csuntry; but within that country their
0C1141948
relative powe:, far fron being aiminished, is greater
than it ever was. Therefore it is logical for them to
FILED
think in terms of rebuildin, from the still intact
foundations, as much us tuey can of the shaken super-
structure, maintainin: anu reaffirming as far as pos-
sible the old proportions nd relative vaiues.
For America, and for the United Nations, tho over-
all problem of the second phase is now to encourage the?
remaking of Japun int) a country in whisn there is
a reasonable prospect of evolving an economic order
RECうRDS
racy. And the probl en is not only to initiate free
enterprise und political democracy, but to axe sure
that they have as much survivul value es possible.
Directives

=== Page 488 === (confidence 0.99)
119
-2-
Directives aimed at bringing about the desired char
will necessarily bear unequally on different seetions e
the propulation. To some they will mean the ourtailing
of old special advantages. To others they will mean the
opening of new opportunities. Those who are hurt will
ory out; but will those who are benefited, or potentially
benefited, rejoice audibly? Not necessarily. Long
repression has left many people politically timid. Even
though a SCAP ordinance may be welcomed by them, they will,
in their present mood, hesitate to take up promptly the
advantages offered to them. The most prevalent fears
which inhibit them are: How far will SCAP eventually go
with the directives it is issuing, and how long will soAP
stay in Japan? If SCAP does not go far enough, before
leaving, or if SCAP leaves too soon, they fear that the
old order may resume full sway in Japan and take revenge
on those who acted too hastily on what seemed to be the
implications or suggestions of the SCAP direotives.
The logical approach would appear to be to issue
direotives which, when they necessarily curtail the inter-
ests of a group capable of vocal protest and political
evasion or resistance, also elicit active enthusiasm and
support, instead of mere passive wait-and-see assent,
from some other group. If a directive opens up unmis-
takeable opportunities to a new group interest, that
group is likely to feel the need to organize politically
in order to protect and extend its newly acquired interest.
Only on some such basis as this can we expect a healthy
political life to develop in Japan. Until the political
"reform" parties are likely to represent little more than
pious formulae. A workable democratic system requires
competitive group interests represented by competing
parties and competitive programs.
2. The Meiji Rescript on Education.
The following is not intended as criticism of General
MacArthur's program. He has an excellent staff seotion
working on the problems of education. I believe, however,
that it would be to the general good if the Department of
State were independently to advise action on these
problems.
The Meiji Rescript on Education has been as much a
fountainhead of authoritarianism in Japan as the privileged
position of the Army and Navy. Even if the present high
degree of centralization in Japanese education were to be
amended by measures of decentralization (and I myself do
not believe decentralization to be of primary importanoe)
Japanese education will remain vulnerable to authoritarian
thought-control unless the Resoript on education is repealed.
The

=== Page 489 === (confidence 0.98)
120
-3-
The initiative in repealing the Reseript on educatios
should come, and come openly, from American polioy. Other
wise there is the danger either that a Japanese-worded
repeal would not clearly enough condemn use of the
imperial aura to justify authoritarian thought-control,
or that the rank and file of teaohers and professors would
not get up the courage to teach and praotice freedom of
K
thought in education.
3. Some Brief Notes on China.
In foreseeing the end of the war in Asia and the
Pacifio the Japanese out-guessed us in one important
respect. We undertook great preparations to make coastal
China a major theater of military action in the final
phase of the war. The Japanese, knowing that they would
probably sucoeed in ending the war by a relatively digni-
fied surrender, concentrated on making China a major
theater of future, post-war political operations.
The Japanese won, to the following extent:
They kept up produotion, especially in the Shanghai
area, enough to prevent critical industrial unemployment.
They managed to keep up relatively good distribution of
goods -- better than in Free China. They enforced a
relatively efficient control of prices.
When surrender came -- as a result of negotiations
in distant Japan, not of visible defeat in the field in
China -- the Chinese Government viotory bandwagon rolled into
Shanghai, Peiping, and Tientsin, wi th an overflowing load
of Kuomintang party stalwarts. Industrial produotion
ceased. Distribution was paralyzed. The Party stalwarts
assembled the Chambers of Commerce and assessed them for
cash levies. Then they turned the merchants loose on the
public, wi th permission to get their money back.
Result: The high-sounding words "Victory" and
"Liberation" have acquired, in Shanghai and elsewhere,
the connotations of stagnant production, unemployment,
skyrocketing prices, and new taxes and impositions.
These connotations are derogatory to the National Govern-
ment and to the Americans as the most conspicuous Allies
of the National Government.
4. A New Crisis in Inner Mongolia.
In Peiping, I had a long conversation with Teh Wang,
who was at one time the most important Mongol nationalist
leader in Inner Mongolia. Later he was made conspicuous
as

=== Page 490 === (confidence 0.99)
121
-4
as the most important figurehead of Japanese costrol of
the Mongols of Inner Mongolia. He has now been listed
by the Chinese Communists as a war oriminal -- a kind
of Potain.
It is my belief, however, that Teh Wang was never a
willing collaborator of the Japanese. He was always on
good terms with Chiang Kai-shek. The failure, in the
1930's, to set up an Inner Mongolian autonomy that could
serve as a back-fire against Japanese propaganda is
attributable primarily to the inability of Chiang Kai-
shek to control the Chinese provincial generals who had
a vested interest in exploiting the Mongols -- esped ally
by expropriating their pasture lands. As a result of
the local pressure of the Chinese on the Mongols, the
Mongols were thrown into the arms of the Japanese. Teh
Wang did not lead the Mongols of Inner Mongolia over to
the side of the Japanese. He merely went with them,
when there was no where else to go.
Teh Wang's behavior at the time of Japan's col-
lapse, and Teh Wang's views at the present time, should
therefore be of partioular interest.
When Japan surrendered, Teh Wang was in Kalgan.
His return to his own territory in Inner Mongolia was
apparently barred by the military situation. He may well
have been equally unwilling to fall into Russian or
Outer Mongolian hands, by trying to get to his home in
Inner Mongolia, or into Chinese Communist hands, by
staying in Kalgan. At any rate, he escaped to Peiping.
From there he was flown to Chungking, where he saw
President Chiang. Because of all this, Teh Wang has
been put on the "wanted" list of war oriminals by the
Chinese Communists.
Teh Wang is in a situation in which he might easily
be seized and suddenly killed not only by the Chinese
Communists, or by one or another group of Mongol revolu-
tionaries, but by the Chinese Government, or one of its
agencies, because in spite of his good personal relations
with President Chiang, he still stands for a kind of Mongol
nationalism which is opposed and feared by many Chinese.
At the same time, Teh Wang still has bargaining power, and
might yet come to the fore again as a figure acceptable,
under some kind of compromise agreement, to the Chinese
Government, the Chinese Communists, the Outer Mongolian
Government, and the Russians.
The following is a ∞ncise recapitulation of what
Teh Wang had to say:
a) He

=== Page 491 === (confidence 0.99)
122
-5-
a) He believes that his son and heir (now nemmis
age of 30, I believe) may have escaped into Outer
If so, Teh Wang may be able to present a case to the out
Mongoiian Government.
b) Teh Wang is certain that there will be an Inner
Mongolian movement for secession from China and, eventuallt
for union with Outer Mongolia. Such a movement would be
popular in his own part of Inner Mongolia (northern fringes
of Suiyuan and Chahar) and, he believes, even more popular
in Eastern or Manchurian Inner Mongolia (northern Jehol;
western fringes of Liaoning, Kirin, and Heilungkiang).
c) Such a Mongol movement would obvdously oreate a
situation favorable to Russia; but in Teh Wang's positive
opinion the genesis of the movement will be local and
Mongol, not Russian.
d) The Mongol hereditary aristocracy, to which Teh
Wang himself belongs, has always been hostile to Outer
Mongolia, but will not oppose this new movement. Teh
Wang said in so many words that there is no politioal hope
in any movement which aims to preserve Mongol feudalism.
e) The only way to forestall the kind of pan-Mongol
movement predicted by Teh Wang is, in his opinion, to throw
together all ongol-inhabited areas in Chahar, Suiyuan,
Jehol, and Western Manchuria and make them into one pro-
vince, with a great deal of autonomy ("like Canada").
The difficulty in carrying through such a measure is
that it would be opposed by the Chinese provincial authori-
ties and provincial militarists, who still stubbornly
insist that the way to deal with the Mongols of Inner
Mongolia is to keep them broken up into "reservations",
each one isolated in a corner of some Chinese province.
In view of the importance of the foregoing -- which
is capable of ramifying far beyond the area immediately
affected -- I venture some specific suggestions.
a) Assign an American to familiarize himself immedi-
ately with Inner Mongolian problems and personalities.
Colonel William Mayer, at present I believe G2 to
Gene ral Wedemeyer, has suttable background experience.
b) Reestablish as soon as possible American consular
representation in Manohuria and at such cities as Kalgan;
establish new consulates at Kueisui (Suiyuan), Chihfeng
(Jehol), Taonan (Lioning) and Hailar (Heilungkiang).
c) Take

=== Page 492 === (confidence 0.98)
o) Take adveatage of the recognision 
independence by China to extend American ree
Outer Mongolia. Establish consular represent
preferably diplomatic representation, at Ulan
It is not suggested that the foregoing measures wonld
solve the problems which we may ∞nfidently expeot to
arise along the Mongolian frontiers; but it is urged that
we should have listening posts, we should know what is
going on, and we should be in a position to extend among
the Mongol peoples the prestige which we already have as
the most important nation in the international relations
of Russia and China.

1. OSHTA
Because of his previous background OSHIMA was no diplomat, but a saldi
and at heart never overcame this past. He only thought in military
consideration and had little interest on understanding for the bigger and
more intricate problems of foreign policy. Like most officers of the
Japanese Army, OSHITMA was convinced that it was war which was the decisive
influence in politics. To me ho has repeatedly expressed the opinion that
the only driving force of Japanese history in modern times and the only really
decisive factor in the Japanese rise (in home and external spheres) since
1858 was the Japanese Army. He does not acknowledge that Japanese industry
played any part in the rise of Japan since the middle of the last century.
In disoussions he was alvays of the opinion that the Japanese asoent was due
to the succcssful vars of the period. Only in support of these wars were
industry and culture ablc to participatç in the reconstruotion and
development of modern Japan and then they certainly were of valuable help:
but the dccisive factors were only the Army and the wars.
This fundamental attitude of OSHIMA's may also help to explain why
he showed such a remarkably favourable disposition towards National Soxialsim
in the .Third Reich.
No doubt OSHIMA's conviction of the great value of
National Socialism was quite genuine, because he defended it with energy
against all sceptics who were fairly stron ly represented in his Embassy. He
was literally fascinated by personalities such as HITLER, GOERING and HIMLER
and his attitude towards thom vas one devoid of criticism. He often went
to the Fuehrer's Headquurters ith the firm intention of putting before the
Fuchrer, on the orders of his Government, some criticisms by the Japanese
Government regarding certain questions of the conmunal var offort and to
explain these to him. As a rul the intorview with the Fuehrer tok the
course that he interruptod OsiIMA abruptly and then gave him a long lecture
during which the Corman attitud is presented as the only correot one and the
Japanese attitude as incorroct. In all the e cnses OSHIMA returned from the
Fuchrer's Headquarters as a "convert". This vas often related to me, not
only by OSHIMA's first secretary but also by his first Botschaftsrat. The
leading officials of th Jap nese Embassy vero extremely disconcerted by
OShHTMA's lack of discrimination and by his servility towards the Fuchrer and
Fuchrer's Headquarters, and never hesitated to voic: their anxiety. Within
the Embassy they called OSHIMA in a jocular manner "Pg Otto". Even in April
the attache Oga told me "OSHIMA will still belicve  a German victory even
when the Russian tanks are standin in front of the Embassy in the Tiergarten".
The result of OSHIMA's unqualificd support of National Socialism and its
leading personalities was a certain enstragoment fron his Embassy Staff.
The majority of the latter were experienced diplomatic officials who were at
least somewhat more reserved in thcir judgement about National Socialiin
owing to their numerous observations and comparisons in other countries.
Therefore they did not share OShTMA's unbounded admiration.
Somehow the Japanese forein Officc had realized comparatively carly
(during the time of proparation for the Tripartite pact) that OstTMA's reports
about the position in Germany were unr liable in their contents and especially
not critioal enough. The Forcigm Office secured, thercfor, information
about the internal position in Gormany through other channels - during the
last few years (until October 1944)-by means of a particularly clever and
experienced professional diplomat who was sent to Berlin ith a faked special
mission and who was included in the Embassy Staff, although he was officially
not under OSHIMA's orders - his direct communication with Tokio not being
controlled by OSHIMA. The last special cmissary" of this kind was SAKUMA
who was recalled to TokIo in October or November of last ycar. to report, but
was held up in Stookholm (as far as I kno:/ until the cnd of the war).
The military attache and thc Naval attache also regarded themselves
as superior to OSHIMA becausc they did not refrain from criticism of National
Sooialism and the German Government.
Far beyond their real soope as
attaohes of. the armed orces, they also reported to TokIo through their own
channels and with their own codes about purely political matters whioh they
observed in Germany in terms quite different from Oshima's reports.

SOURCES OF INFORMATION OF THE FAR EASTERN DEPARTMENT
OF THE OKW AND RSHA
1.
Abwehrstelle Shanghai.
A special report has already been made about this branch.
Supplementary details are as follows:-
Information coming fron Shanghai was first of all evaluated by
the Japanese Group (Japangruppe) in the Amt. Mil. of the RSHA-Oberstleu-
tnant (Lt/Col) OHLETZ, Major BAECHTLE and Hauptmann PLAGE. To this
Group also belonged a Fahrenjunker Feldwebel by the name of BRENNsChEIDT
who had a far greater expert knowledge of the subject than all the other.
members of the Group, as he had been a merthant in China for a long time
and thus was able to gain his experience on the spot.
After having been dealt with by this Group of the Amt Mil of the
RSHA the Shanghai reports were distributed to the various interested
Agencies.
2.
Intelligence Informants in Germany:
(i) Admiral Richard FOERSTER, Berlin President of the German-
Japanese Society. FOERSTER had friandly relations with OSHIMA
and also with most of the leading menbers of the Embassy Staff.
He never made written reports about his conversations with the
Japanese; however, from time to time he was visited by repre-
sentatives of all Agencies who were interested in Japanese
questions and hc vwillingly passed on information about anything
he might have learned fron the Japanese. He met Ambassador
OSIIMA comparatively frequently and got to know from him details
about OSIIMA's visits to the Führer's Headquarters. The value
of Intelligencc infonaation derived from FOERSTER was not very
high.
(ii) Assessor TROEMEL. TROEMEL was general secretary of the
German-Japanese Society in BERLIN and thus came frequently into
contact with members of the Japanese Embassy and the Japanese
colony. He was particularly well inforned about the feeling of the
younger members of the Embassy and the Japanese journalists. He
himself was more or less disinterested politically and therefore did
not deliberately collect infomation, nor was he acquinted with
the complicated details of the political conditions in Eastem Asia.
His infornation was therefore of no decisive value.
(iii) Herr ZANDER. ZANDER originally was a book-seller and Publisher;
besides this he was privately used by OSHIMA for writing the German text
of the letters which OSHIMA sent out as answers to invitations, thanks
for.gifts, etc. Thus Z NDER came into close contact with OSHIMA and
naturally used this opportunity to start political conversations. Not
much useful information was gained from these conversations, anyhow
not enough for somebody who was not acquainted with the political
situation in East Asia. The bweka and RSHA regarded Z/NDER as a
"V-Mann" however, not within the East Asia Department but within mt
VI A, which dealt as far as I know with internal German administrative
affairs. Thus I, too, got to know him superficially. He lived in
BERLIN - Nikolassce, I do not know the street. It seems that Z4NDER
was also ordcred by some agency to keep in close ∞ntact with Swiss
jourmalists living in BERLIN, and I presume that this order repres-
ented his main activity as a "V-Mann". ny Eastern siatio information
he produced from
his contact with OSHIMA was neither substantial
nor new.

=== Page 507 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
(iv) Dr. DONiT. DON.T was professor of "Japanology" at
Auslandswissenschaftliohen (forcign-scientific) faoulty of
Berlin University. This faculty was regarded amongst the.
professors of the university as a more or less disguised 
Branch of the SD. Therefore DONAT was also closely oollabox
with the RSH4, whioh aleo provided him with a speoial "East
Institute" where DONAT employed a number of Far Eastem Eixperts
as assistants on the study of scientific subjeots which seemed
worth while evaluating because of their Intelligence Interest.
DONAT also used some foreigners there, amongst them a Dutah
specialist for Duttoh East India questions and also a Russian-
Mongolian expert. The names of these men I cannot remember
now without the help of my notes. DONT himself also partioipated
in thc evaluation of Intelligence subjects. Besides that he
cultivated social contacts with all members of Eastern Lsiatio
Nations who were represented in BERLIN, in particular with Indd-
Chinese students who had come to BERLIN from FRiNCE. At first
his Institute was situated in the Oranienburger Strasse where it
was bombed out last utumn. From there it moved to Marienbad

=== Page 512 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure to despatch No. 343 dated April 3, 1946, frem the
Office of the United States Political Adviser, Tokyo. Jayem.
on the subjeet "Political Parties in Japan: Develepments
During the Week Ending March 30, 1946."
OFFICE OF THE UNITED STATES POLITICAL ADVISER
Tokyo, Japan, April 2, 1946
CONPIDENTIAL
POLITICAL PARTIES IN JAPAN: DEVELOPMITS DURING
THE WEEK ENDING MARCH 30, 1946
fummary. Within their capabilities, the various political
parties and their candidates are making an effort to arouse the
interest and enthusiasm of the electorate in their respective
platforms. Despite limitations brought on by shortages of
mterials, difficulties of travel and communications, and r
gulations concerning posters, the tempo of the campaign is is-
creasing in the cities, towns, and villages of Japan. A feeling
of pessimism over the lack of public interest in the electiom
nevertheless pervades various circles. Reasons for this apparent
failure on the part of the people to respond to the freedoms of
discussion, congregation, and voting power may be attributed to
the hardships and inconveniences of daily life, lack of confi-
dence in the parties, leadership, and candidates, fatalism of
the Japanese masses, low levels of political education and
experience of the people, and to some extent, the ineptness
of the parties in arousing public enthusiasm. Taken in combi-
nation, these various factors suggest that abstention of voters
in the coming election will be high, a result which would be
unfortunate and might give rise to an implication that the
initial test of democracy in Japan has failed. The Government
is aware of this danger. If the new Diet is to represent the
choice of the Japanese people as a whole, abstention from
voting must be kept to a minimum. To combat this disinterested-
ness, it may perhaps be desirable for SCAP, through suitable
channels and means, to emphasize the importance of the politi-
cal responsibilities which the people have to assume under
these new freedoms and which must be discharged in the election
and in the new Diet. Somewhat in keeping with the practice of
political parties in other countries, when dealing with such
specific problems as inflation, Governmental finance, unemploy-
ment, and the food shortage, the programs of the major parties
are made up of generalities. In some instances, however,
Japanese parties have exceeded practices elsewhere in showing
an inclination to juggle inconsistencies, to ignore objectivity,
and to confuse or dismiss fundamental issues with political
"mumbo-jumbo". As of March 31, a total of 2,787 candidates
were running in the campaign. End of Summary.
The Blection Campaign
With the election campaign now entering into ite final
phase, the various political parties and their candidates are
making an effort, within their capabilities, to arouse the
interost

=== Page 513 === (confidence 0.98)
2
interest and enthusiasm of the electorate in their respective
platforms. The pattern of campaigning in Japan is tame by com-
parison with similar activities in the United States, because
of the limitations under present conditions imposed on the
parties in Japan by shortages of materials, diffioulties and
expense of travel and communications, and the detailed regala-
tions concerning permissible sises of posters and advertising
matter. There are, nevertheless, an increasing number of
posters bearing the names of candidates and campaign slogans
making their appearance throughout the cities, towns, and
villages of Japan-evidence that the candidates and khegrise-
porters are busily engaged in furthering their causes. Less
obvious, but probably more efficacious in gathering adherents,
are the speaking tours conducted in the provinces by candidates
who thereby hope to popularise themselves with their constitu-
ents. Tours of this nature are usually arranged by a committee
which fixes a rigid schedule of appearances throughout the pre-
fecture, it being not unusual for a candidate to attend as masy
as fifty "meetings" over a period of five or six days. In
addition, the candidate attends the customary private entertain-
ments, such as lunches and dinners, which afford ample opportuaity
for back-stage discussion of campaign problems and strategy.
Despite the activities of the candidates and their support-
ere, however, a feeling of pessimism over the lack of publie
interest in the election campaign appears to pervade Government
circles, politicians, and many thoughtful Japanese. Lack of
enthusiasm for the election among the general populace, par
ticularly in the large cities, is such that the Home and Mdu-
cation Ministries are making an effort to stimulate greater
interest, but as yet with little or no success, and with un-
determined effect on the total vote to be cast.
In searching for underlying reasons for the apparent
failure of the Japanese to respond to their newly-found free-
doms of discussion, congregation, and voting power, the hard-
ships and insonveniences of daily life, including difficulties
of travel and communications, are the chief factors which de-
tract from political activity and interest. Contributory
causes, powerful but less obvious, are (a) a general lack of
confidence in the parties, leadership, and candidates: (b)
the deep-seated fatalism inherently characteristic of the
Japanese masses, accustomed to follow but not to lead; (c)
the low levels of political education and experience of the
people as a whole; and (3) to some extent, the ineptness of
the parties in arousing public enthusiasm for their respective
principles.
Candidates and the Diet
In evaluating the standing and character of the numerous
candidates, even well-informed Japanese observers are all too
apt to dismiss the subject with over-simplifications such as
"all candidates are of low caliber", "all politicians are venal".
or "all candidates are merely new faces masking for old." This
attitude of resignation is difficult to combat, in view of the
suspicions

=== Page 514 === (confidence 0.98)
3
suspicions with which politicians in Japan have been viewed for
many years. It is also apparent that many of the purged peliti-
cal leaders of Japan are generally known or believed to be the
sponsors of substitute candidates now running under the auspices
of a major party label.
Taken in combination, the various factors touched upon above
are giving rise to predictions by competent Japanese observers
that the abetention of voters in the election will be high, and
that the forthcoming Diet may not be chosen by a real mjority
of the people. A growth of this belief, prior to Japan s first
post-hostilities general election, would be unfortunate beomse
of possible implications that the initial test of democracy in
Japan has failed and that the same leaders of the political
parties are still in power in the background. (It is to be
noted that reference is not mede here to the former leaders of
Japan who have been discredited, who have retired, or who have
otherwise disappeared from important political activity. Re-
ference is made merely to important figures in political parties
and in the Diet.)
Aside from the political importance of the forthcoming
general election as a manifestation of political procedure
under American democratization policies in Japan, it is apparent
that the new Diet should, if possible, represent the choice of.
the Japanese people as a whole, a choice which will be imcontestable
only if the rate of abstention from voting is kept to a mimimum.
While it is true that education, time, and greater politieal ex-
perience are the ingredients from which a Japanese democrecy
must be shaped, popular interest might perhaps be inereased by
giving encouragement to the candidates as a class. The stigme
of "low caliber" which has been attached to many candidates has
simultaneously undermined the confidence of the people in the
Diet. Not all candidates are bad, and it is to be hoped that
new leaders of character and competence will arise.
It is believed highly important to take every appropriate
step to combat disinterestedness of the Japanese people in poli-
tical affairs and their innate suspicion and low opinion of
party politicians and party politics as a whole. It might be
especially effective to stress the necessity for every qualified
voter to cast his or her vote. Woman suffrage will in all
likelihood increase considerably the percentage of abstainers
unless efforts to bring women to the polls are successful. In
this connection it would appear helpful and desirable for SCAP
to employ the prestige and influence of the occupation to
emphasise, through suitable channels and means, the importance
of the political responsibilities which the people must assume
under their new freedom and which they must discharge in the
election and in the new Diet. The candidates, also, might well
receive encouragement from a statement that the new Diet will
have sympathetic understanding and assistance of scar, provided
that genuine efforts are made to develop truly democratic prin-
ciples in Japan.
carront

=== Page 515 === (confidence 0.99)
Current Problems and the Parties
Published statements, speeches, and radio talks sponsored
by the political parties generally fail to deal specifically
with the major problems with which the Japanese people are
currently faced. Por the most part, radio talks made by major
party candidates are prepared more for listener appeal than
for the advancement of possible solutions of these problems.
while the talks of minor party and independent candidates are
variously poor, ill-prepared, and heavily weighted on the side
of vituperation against traditional politicians. In overall
propaganda approach, the major parties continue to emphasise
the shortcomings of Government policies to a point where the
Cabinet's prestige and authority is threatened with being
seriously undermined. However, no concrete alternatives are
offered. There is also a pronounced tendency on the part of
party speakers to denounce rival party principles, a healthy
sign were it not for the sterility of the speakers' programs.
Finally, in keeping with the known sentiments of the great
majority of the electorate, the maintenance of the Emperor
system is in most cases propounded as an original party prin-
ciple, despite the fact that the Emperor's retention is favor
ed by all and is no longer a live campaign issue, except for
the Communist Party.
Somewhat in keeping with the practice of political parties
in other countries, the parties' respective attitudes on spe-
cific practical problems generally amount to platitudinous
statements of principles, lacking substance, and generally
further confusing the issue by their contradiction of terms.
Thus, on the important problem of combatting inflation, the
Progressives advocate an adjustment of State revenue and ex-
penditure, but desire generous funde for social and cultural
programs. The Social Democrats, while advocating nationaliza-
tion of leading industries as a check to inflation, fail to
demonstrate that production will be thereby increased. The
Communist plan for an anti-inflation policy is almost entirely
based upon Communistic ideology and would have hope of success
only if Japan as a whole were Communized. All parties advocate
increased food production as one of the methods for halting
inflationary trends yet their suggestions lack practicality or
a objective examination of capabilities.
Governmental financial problems are treated by the
parties in terms of "financial readjustment." Simultaneously,
they propose social and rehabilitation programs complotely at
variance with financial capabilities. Outstanding Government
bonds are recognized in principle by the Progressives and
Liberals, while the Social Democrats and Communists favor can-
cellation of the holdings of large bondholders. Internal war
indemnity payments are advocated by the Progressives and Liberals,
as opposed to cancellation of all indemaification proposed by
the Social Democrats and Communists. The latter two parties
apparently also desire to change Japan's economic order irrespective
of any confusion which might result therefrom.
sovora1

=== Page 516 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
Several parties would solve the unemployment problem by
such methods as the promotion of export industries and the
construction of public works, plans which are somewhat in-
consistent with their advocacy of "financial readjustment" and
rely to some extent upon the principle of giving jobs to end
unemployment. Whether Japan would be financially capable of
supporting large-scale deficit financing is a question which
the parties do not answer.
Various solutions of the serious problems of food produc-
tion and distribution are suggested by the parties. The Pro-
gressives approve of governmental coercive measures but seek
assistance from SCAP. The Liberal Party merely insists that
coercive methods to force farmers to sell their rice are fruit-
less and adds vaguely that in any event, rice is scarce. The
food problem is used by the Social Democrats as an argument for
increasing the membership of the new Japan Farmers' Union, while
the Communists sponsor voluntary offerings for sale of rice
through the Communiet-controlled Farmers' Committees. The
Cooperatives advocate voluntary sales of rice by farmers, but
suggest that no rice is available.
Candidates and the Parties
As of March 3l, the net mumber of candidates continuing
in the campaign showed a decrease of one. Six Liberals and
nine Social Democratic candidates withdrew from the race,
while the number of "independents" increased from 788 to 802.
The respective mumbers of candidates by parties, compared with
those of a week ago, are as follows:

=== Page 521 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 2 to despatch No. 350 dated April 5. 1946, frem
the Office of the United States Political Adviser, Tekyo. Jagem.
on the subject "Ordinance Governing Certification of Cendidates
for House of Representatives."
TRANSLATION
(From the Official Gasette, January 30, 1946)
Home Affairs Hinistry Ordinance No. 2
In accordance with the provisions of Imperial Ordinance
No. 542 of 1945, issued in consequence of the acceptance of the
Potedam Declaration, the following provisions are made concern-
ing the certification of eligibility of a person desiring to
become a candidate for election to the House of Representatives.
30th of January, 1946.
MITSUCHI Chuso
Minister of Home Affairs
1. A person who, in the forthcoming general election of
the House of Representatives, desires to announce his candidacy
or to make a nomination (hereafter termed an announcement and
a nomination), in accordance with the provisions of Paragraphs
1 and 2 of Article 67 of the House of Representatives Mection
Law, may request a certification of the Minister of Home
Affaire to the effect that the candidate-to-be is not a
person included under the provisions of the 4th of January
1946 Directive of the Supreme Commander of Allied Powers,
relating to the removal and exclusion of undesirable persons
from public office.
2. A person desiring the certification in the previous
article, should petition immediately the Minister of Home
Affairs through the prefectural Govemer having juriedictien
over the residence of the candidate, accompanying such peti-
tion with four copies of a statement of the personal history
of the candidate-to-be, in accordance with Form 1 appended
bereto.
3. When the Minister of Home Affairs, in consultation
with the Prime Minister, has certified that the candidate-to-
be is not a person affected by the memorandum referred to in
Article l, a written certification, in conformance with form
No. 2 appended hereto shall be dispatched.
If in the statement referred to in the previous article
which is submitted by the candidate-to-be himself, a false
assertion is made or a fact concealed, this certification
shall be null and void.
Supplemental Provision
This ordinance shall become effective on the date of
promulgation.

SUBJECT: Campaign Literature of the Progressive Party

I have the honor to forward under separate cover a single
complete set of campaign literature prepared by the matiemal
headquarters of the Progressive Party for distribution te ite
recognised candidates in the forthcoming general election.
Limitation of time and personnel has prevented translation of
this voluminous material, but it is believed that it will be
of interest to analytical branches of the Department in
original form. It is understood that ORI has indicated
DC/R
particular desire for its receipt.
From the contents of the material it will be noted that
the headquarters of the Progressive Party has made an attempt
Ay2  1345
to brief its candidates theroughly on the Party's attitudes
on the major current problems: preservation of the "national
REF
polity"; revision of the Constitution; maintenance of public
order; anti-inflation measures; educational reform; and food
supply. Texts of illustrative speeches by representative
Party leaders are furnished as a guide to the candidates; and
detailed instructions and information concerning the opera-
tion of the Election Law are included.
An effort is being made to obtain similar material from
the other major parties.
Respectfully yours,

○食糧緊急對策要綱昭二三四
一、緊迫せる食糧問題の解決が現下凡ゆる經濟施策の基盤を爲し且之に先行すべき緊念間題なるとと
二之が解決策に付ては二十年産米の事情に鑑み具體的事貴に依り食ねの先行き不安特に紹對量不足に謝する不安と
解消一國民に安心感を與ふること
三施策の遂行に當り務めて強権の發動を避くるとと
を前提とし急速にた配對策の貴行を促進せんとす。
配
１、資金資材原料勞力、輸送力等の重點使用の決定。
食緊意對策途行の爲必要なる責金、資材、原料、勞力、離送力等に付ては政府は闘係方面の諒解と協力を得て
優先的に之が使用を決定し直に實行に移すこと
２い需給推算上の絶對不足量の輸人。
二十年産米の實收狀況に艦み供給量の不足は飽迄も之を転入に依て補墳し、國民の不安を解消するの要あるを以
て、速に聯合同側の諒解を得、輸入の實行に付許可を得ること、尙此の場合輪入業務の實際は民間商社の滑動に
待つとととし適當なる機構を新設するとと
8、供出米制度の改善。
供出米成頼不良の現況に鑑み之が根本改革を必要とするも食ね危機に在る今日直に之が改愛を行ふととは更に漏
亂を生するの惧れあるを以て感意對策として一應現在の供出制度を認め、之が促進を期する言速に左の慮量を勝
すること
イ）農家に對する供出割當數量を減額して大體小作米として收納する數量程度とし供出米の買上げ慣格は
り五百圓程度とするとと
（口）供出完了後の農家手持の米に付ては自由販変を認め其の販賣は生産地府縣に正米市場を設け之を通じて
買でしむるとと、従て若し供出割當数量の買入のみに依ては都市等に於ける基準配給数量を充し得さる場会
其い数量に付では政府亦之を正米市場を通じて買取るものとするとと
4、二重似格制の創設。
供出米買入價格の引上げ及自由取引市場の設定等に依り一時的た生活困難を來すべを動勞者年收五千圖以下
世帯の所要數量に付ては販変價格を一升二圓五拾錢以下とし、其の他の者に動する所要數量に付ては政府の
入價格に一定歩合を加算したる價格を以て販賣すること
5、配給制度の改善
（イ）政府の責任に屬する基準配給量の範圍に於て前記二重價格制度を採用するの外、國民生活必冊品工業及之
に闘郡する基礎産業等に從事する者に對し重點配給を爲すこと
（口）配給機構に付ては根本的に考究を要するも、生産地に於ける蒐荷、配給販賣の機闘としては農業會等の
機構を活用し都會地に於ける末端配給所の機構は、必要ある場合は、地域的又は職場的消費組合を結成せし
めて之が管理下に留くか又は現配給所の人的物的設備を消費組合内に移すとと
6農業經營用品の製造工業及國民生活必需物資生産工業の再建。
肥料、農機具、農難藥劑作業衣、地下足絞等の農業經營用品並に農家の生活必需物賁を確實に且嚴領化入手せ
しむるととは米麥等の増産及之が供出を促進する上に絶對必要録件たるに鑑み、速に之等物資の生産工業の再
を期する爲緊念に左の虚置を採るとと
（イ）之等物資の製造工場の新設、復舊若は補修又は之が運轉等の為に要する責金原料、資材又は勞力等を
、先的に敵地配格又は買入の授助を爲すとと
（ロ）必要ある揚合は之等工場に謝し食糧の時別醜給を爲すとと
（へ）之等工場製品の領格に付ては飲府及消費者側に於て痛當なる曲量と取引上痛當なる考識を拂はしむるた
？、交通通信の鉴備充實。

○敗戰處理及惡性インーシヨン緊急對策要綱
（昭二二二五發）
我は、風に敗戦応理並に復興の爲、現に多くの財産を有する者又は戦争彼害の少なかりし者が、特に多くの負
をなすべきものなりとい信念をもい従っい未會有い戦禍を利せる財産増加の如き、断じて之を放し得さるや
論無きととうない加ふるに問民結済を崩壊より救ひ、健全なる國家財政を再建するが爲には、今日以上の通貨の
張は之を阻止するい必安きるのみならす、富の喪失に表裏して山積せるた大なる國家債務を現狀のまへに放任するを
得す。此の趣旨に於我黨い敗戦魔理並に悪性インフレーションへの緊急對策婚綱を決定するとと左の如し
第一、生活必語品特に食糧の増産並に輸入への格段の努力。
インフレの悪性化を防ぐには通貨面よりの對策のみを以てしては不充分にして、それにも増して重要なるは生話
必需、特に食種の供給を確保するととなり
（說明は別項食糧緊急對策要綱並に企業再建緊念對策要綱に讓る）
第二二
1、通貨發行高制限への即時復歸。
今日の如く毎日一俺圓乃至二催圓にも及ぶ通貨膨張の狀況を持顔しつへィンフレの悪性化を防止することは絶謝
に不可能なるを以て少くとも今日以上に通貨を膨張せしむるととは萬難を排して回避せさるべからすと思料す
2、新公債發行の停止。
、公債の元利拂の延期。
インフレの悪性化を防止すべを當面至上の要動に恵する爲には、新公債の發行を停止するは勿論、従来の右大
る公僕の元利拂を棟くるととをも亦断然之を証期するととは真に已むを待さるところなり。
4、軍冊會社並に外地財産に動する政府補賀の凍結。
5、戦爭利得に對する特別課税。
6、十萬圓以上の財産を有するものに謝する「圓民連帯税」の創数、似し戦災を数りたる者並に戦歿者の貴書に謝
しては発税點及親事の上に相當の考意をなす
7、官事務の基理による行政費の徹底的削減並に思給制度の根本的改革。
第三、最爭利得に對する特別課税並に國民連帶稅の收入は國民生活の安定、産業の再建職災復興失業對策の為
經費に光當す。
○企業再建緊急對策要綱昭二二二五發表
遠に企業の再建方覚を樹立し、産業貴本家に對しては企業の嚮ふべき途を明示して欣然生産増強に精進せしめ、従
員勞務者に對しては之が職場を痛保して失業對覚の一助たらしめ、以て国民生活に必要なる物資の急速増産を期する
は民生安定上喫緊の要務なるのみならず又以て悪性ィンフレ防止の爲、積極的且不可缺の對策なり。之が為我黨の採
らんとする實行方賛の要綱概ね左の如し。
一、速に國民生活安定上必要なる物資の生産工業並に之に調聯する账礎産業の種類及規模を決定し、之を再建すべき
企業として明示するとと。
二、右に依り決定したる企業に對しては、之が再建に必要なる工場設備、機械設備等の補修又は新設に付、貴金、責
材勇力、輸送力等を優先的に使用せしむるとと
三既存の車譜工業其の他職時緊要工業に使用せる工場設備、機械設備、勞力、資材等は、右決定に基く諸産業に
轉用し、光分に其の数用を發揮せしむる様鹿置するとと。
四、存の軍冊工業其他職時素要工業に使用せる工場設備等の用に際し、之等軍需工業等の企業が負婚せる債務の
清算に付ては別途講理する方策を樹立し工場設備等は新企業再建の爲、速に使用せしむる様處置するとと。
五、再建すべき企業に付ては、其の企業主慢に對し金融機闘をして戦時中に於ける債横債務の魔理狀況の如何を間は
す積極的に設備責金、運轉賞金の敵通を為さしむるとと。
六、之等再遺金業の製品慣格に付て、米便を基準とする新物價水準に比例して一定價格を協定せしめ、其の便格を以
て償ひ得さる生産費に謝しては国家が補償かるとと。
七急連再建を要すべを企業の種類は遠に官民一體の企業再意委員會を設置し、興論に稽み大局的見地に立ちて之が
順位と意親を決定するとと。
